Back to Boundaries in Billiards
Leonid A. Bunimovich, Yaofeng Su

Abstract
We prove Poisson limit laws for open billiard systems with holes in the boundary of billiard tables. Traditionally some abstract holes in the phase space of a billiard were studied. Holes in the boundary are of an intrinsic interest for billiard systems, especially for applications. Sinai billiards with or without a finite horizon, diamond billiards, and semi-dispersing billiards are considered. However, the emphasis is on focusing billiards with slow decay of correlations, where various new technical difficulties arise.



∎

1
Introduction

The studies of Poisson approximations for recurrences to small subsets in the phase spaces of chaotic dynamical systems are developed now into a large active area. Another view at this type of problems is a subject of the theory of open dynamical systems, where some positive measure subset of the phase space is named a hole, and the process of escape through the hole is studied.

In a general setup, one picks a small measure subset (a hole) in the phase space of a hyperbolic (chaotic) ergodic dynamical system and attempts to prove that in the limit, when the measure of the hole approaches zero, the corresponding process of recurrences to the hole converges to a Poisson process.

This area received an essential boost after L-S.Young papers
Young 1998
,
Young 1999
, where a new general framework was introduced for analysis of statistical properties of hyperbolic dynamical systems. This approach employs a representation of the phase space of a dynamical system as a tower (later called a Young tower, a Gibbs-Markov-Young tower, etc), which allows to study dynamics by analysing recurrences to the base of the tower.
Particularly, in the papers
Pène and Saussol 2020
,
Pène and Saussol 2016
,
Bunimovich and Su 2022
the holes, which are the balls, shrinking to a point in the phase space of billiard systems, were studied. The paper
Haydn and Vaienti 2020
deals with the holes (shrinking to a curve) in the phase space of Sinai billiards with finite horizons. The paper
Pène and Saussol 2010
studied holes within the Sinai’s billiard tables, and the work
Pène and Saussol 2020
considered the holes near corners of a diamond-shaped billiard table. Such holes correspond to strip-shaped holes in the phase space which are shrinking to broken line segments. Also, the paper
Haydn and Vaienti 2020
should be mentioned, which is dealing with various holes shrinking to null sets in the phase space. Some of the systems, which were considered in this paper
Haydn and Vaienti 2020
, can not be modelled by Young towers, but they have milder singularities than the ones in the billiard systems.

Here we present a new development of this area, which, particularly, allows to prove Poisson approximations for various billiard systems with arbitrarily slow decays of correlations. Moreover, we also consider holes located on the boundaries of billiard tables. (Such holes are of special interest for applications, where the holes in the boundary are really made by switching off a particular field generated, e.g., by scanning lasers, and measuring escapes of particles through such holes in the boundary
Friedman et al. 2001
,
Milner et al. 2001
). Our main result can be informally described as presented below (a formal description can be found in Theorem
2.1
).

Theorem.

For a large class of hyperbolic billiards, the processes of hitting and escaping through a hole in the boundary of a billiard table (generated by billiard maps) are asymptotically Poissonian.
∎

The holes within the boundaries are natural to consider for the process of escape in billiard systems. In the phase space such holes tend to straight segments, when a hole within the boundary shrinks to a point. Moreover, exactly such holes are studied in real systems, most notably in physics experiments
Nöckel and Stone 1997
,
Nöckel et al. 1996
,
Richter 2001
,
Friedman et al. 2001
,
Milner et al. 2001
. It should be also mentioned that real experiments in physics dealing with billiards usually consider simply connected (i.e., without inner “holes”) billiard tables. In such experiments the particles (which could be considered as noninteracting between themselves) escape through a hole within the boundary. Then a special (measuring) experimental device counts a number of escaping per unit time particles. It is especially important for quantum chaos experiments because the counting devices must be located outside of billiard tables in order to measure the dynamics of the system, rather than interactions between the system and the measuring device. However, in numerical, rather than real, experiments one can certainly consider a hole of any type. For instance, Sinai billiards and the Lorentz gas have not simply connected billiard tables. We are not familiar with physics experiments where open billiards of this type were studied. One can make a standard formal assumption of the mathematical theory of open systems that when the particle gets into a hole then it “disappears”. (In real experiments the particles after escape do not disappear instantly, but continue to propagate outside the billiard table, and interact with other particles, fields, etc). However, for the sake of generality, we do not assume in the present paper that the billiard tables are simply connected.

In comparison to previous papers we obtain here new results, some of which are a kind of unexpected.

1.

The technique used in
Pène and Saussol 2010
works for Sinai billiards only with finite horizons. Our Theorem
2.1
is applicable to a larger class of billiard systems. The technique used here is also new for general open dynamical systems.

2.

The approach employed in the papers
Pène and Saussol 2010
,
Pène and Saussol 2020
requires to verify the so-called short return conditions specifically for each billiard system, while our main Theorem
2.1
assumes only some natural general, and easy to verify, conditions.

3.

Unlike
Haydn and Vaienti 2020
, Theorem
2.1
shows that, surprisingly, the validity of the Poisson approximation for billiard systems does not depend on fast correlations decay, i.e., it holds for any rate of decay of correlations.

4.

The papers
Pène and Saussol 2016
,
Bunimovich and Su 2022
require that the contracting (resp. expanding) rates along stable (resp. unstable) manifolds must be sufficiently large. Our results show that this condition can be weakened.

The structure of the paper is the following. The section
2
presents some notations, definitions, the formulation of the main Theorem
2.1
, and ideas of the proofs. The sections
3
-
8
contain a proof of this Theorem
2.1
. We start by giving a general result on a Poisson approximation for general point processes. Then we simplify it, step by step, from the section
3
to the section
8
. The section
9
deals with applications to various billiard systems, especially to slowly mixing billiards, which are the main focus in this paper.

2
Definitions, Notations, Main Results, and Ideas of the Proofs

2.1
Definitions, notations and main results

We start by introducing some notations

1.

C_{z}
denotes a constant depending on
z
.

2.

The notation
``a_{n}\precsim_{z}b_{n}"
(
``a_{n}=O_{z}(b_{n})"
) means that there is a constant
C_{z}\geq 1
such that (s.t.)
a_{n}\leq C_{z}b_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
, whereas the notation
``a_{n}\precsim b_{n}"
(or
``a_{n}=O(b_{n})"
) means that there is a constant
C\geq 1
such that
a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
. Next,
``a_{n}\approx_{z}b_{n}"
and
a_{n}=C_{z}^{\pm 1}b_{n}
mean that there is a constant
C_{z}\geq 1
such that
C_{z}^{-1}b_{n}\leq a_{n}\leq C_{z}b_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
. Further, the notations
``a_{n}=C^{\pm 1}b_{n}"
and
``a_{n}\approx b_{n}"
mean that there is a constant
C\geq 1
such that
C^{-1}b_{n}\leq a_{n}\leq Cb_{n}
for all
n\geq 1
. Finally,
``a_{n}=o(b_{n})"
means that
\lim_{n\to\infty}|a_{n}/b_{n}|=0
.

3.

The notation
\mathbb{P}
refers to a probability distribution on the probability space, where a random variable lives, and
\mathbb{E}
denotes the expectation of the random variable.

4.

\mu_{A},\Leb_{A}
denote measures on a set
A
, unless it is specifically mentioned.

5.

\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{A})
denotes a tangent bundle of a (sub)manifold
\mathcal{A}
.

6.

\mathbb{N}=\{1,2,3,\cdots\}
,
\mathbb{N}_{0}=\{0,1,2,3,\cdots\}
.

Definition 1

(Billiard tables, billiard maps and phase spaces)
.

We consider a billiard in a two-dimensional region
Q\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
(called a billiard table) with a piece-wise smooth (of class
C^{3}
) boundary
\partial Q
. Each smooth piece has a uniformly bounded curvature. The boundary
\partial Q
is equipped with a field of inward unit normal vectors
n(q),q\in\partial Q
.

A billiard is a dynamical system generated by the motion of a point
particle with the unit velocity inside the region
Q
being reflected from its
boundary according to the law “the angle of incidence equals the angle of
reflection”. It means that upon reflection the tangent component of the
velocity remains the same, while the normal component changes its sign
according to the rule
v_{+}=v_{-}-2\langle n(q),v_{-}\rangle n(q)
, where
v_{+}
(resp.
v_{-}
) is the velocity of the particle immediately after (resp. before) reflection.

The phase space of a billiard is the restriction of the unit tangent
bundle of
\mathbb{R}^{2}
to
Q
. We will use the standard notation for phase points
x=(q,v)
, where
q
is the point of the configuration space
Q
and
v
is the unit velocity vector. The billiard preserves the Liouville measure
d\nu:=dqdv
where
dq
and
dv
are Lebesgue measures on
Q
and on the unit
one-dimensional sphere. The corresponding flow will be denoted by
\{S^{t}\}
. It is customary for billiard-type systems to study instead of
\{S^{t}\}
a
dynamical system with discrete time, which is called a billiard map
f
. Denote

\mathcal{M}=\{x=(q,v),q\in\partial Q,\langle v,n(q)\rangle>0\}.

For
x=(q,v)\in\mathcal{M}
, let
\tau(x)
be
the first positive moment of reflection from the boundary of the billiard
orbit determined by
x
. Then the billiard map is defined by
f(x)=(q^{\prime},v^{\prime})=S^{\tau}x
, so that
q^{\prime}
is the point of
the next reflection and
v^{\prime}
is the outcoming velocity vector at that point. We call
\mathcal{M}
a phase space of the billiard map
f
.

Due to the regularities of
\partial Q
, the set of singular points of the boundary
\partial Q
is of measure zero, the angle
\phi
of the velocity vector
v
varies from
-\pi/2
to
\pi/2
at any regular point
q\in\partial Q
, hence
\mathcal{M}=\partial Q\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
almost surely. In what follows we always identify
\mathcal{M}
as
\partial Q\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
and denote the phase point
x\in\mathcal{M}
by
(q,\phi)\in\partial Q\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
throughout the paper.

The phase space
\mathcal{M}=\partial Q\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is endowed with a natural Riemannian metric
d_{\mathcal{M}}
and Riemannian volume
\Leb_{\mathcal{M}}
. The billiard map
f
preserves

d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}:=(2\Leb_{\partial Q}\partial Q)^{-1}\cos\phi d\phi dq=(2\Leb_{\partial Q}\partial Q)^{-1}\cos\phi\Leb_{\mathcal{M}},

where
dq
is the one-dimensional Lebesgue measure on the boundary
\partial Q
and

d\phi
is the one-dimensional Lebesgue (uniform) measure on
[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
.

Definition 2

(An induced system)
.

Suppose that there is a fixed subset
X\subseteq\mathcal{M}
with
\Leb_{\mathcal{M}}(X)>0
. The first return time to
X
is
R:X\to\mathbb{N}
. We assume that
X
can be partitioned into countably many connected pieces

X=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}X_{i}\mod{0},

(2.1)

so that
R
is constant on each
X_{i}
and

\Leb_{\mathcal{M}}(\partial X_{i})=0,\quad\interior{X_{i}}\bigcap\interior{X_{j}}=\emptyset\text{ for }i\neq j.

The first return time
R
induces a first return map
f^{R}:X\to X
and a new dynamical system
(X,f^{R})
.

Definition 3

(Singularities and (un)stable manifolds)
.

Denote by
\mathbb{S}\subseteq X
the singularity set for
f^{R}
. For billiard systems,
\mathbb{S}
has zero Lebesgue measure, and
\mathbb{S}^{c}\subseteq X
consists of countably many open connected components. Unstable (resp. stable) manifolds are the connected components of
(\bigcup_{i\geq 0}(f^{R})^{i}\mathbb{S})^{c}
(resp.
(\bigcup_{i\geq 0}(f^{R})^{-i}\mathbb{S})^{c}
). A closed and connected part of the unstable (resp. stable) manifold will be called an unstable (resp. stable ) disk. We denote each unstable (resp. stable) manifold/disk by
\gamma^{u}
(resp.
\gamma^{s}
), and its tangent vectors by
v^{u}
(resp.
v^{s}
).

Remark 1
.

The singularity set
\mathbb{S}
consists of the points in
X
which are not “well-behaved”. It includes the discontinuities and the points where the map
f^{R}
is not differentiable. It may also include other points in
X
with some “bad” properties.

Definition 4

(Chernov-Markarian-Zhang (CMZ) structures)
.

We say that an induced system
(X,f^{R})
in Definition
2
is a CMZ structure of the billiard system
(\mathcal{M},f)
if there are constants
C>0
and
\beta\in(0,1)
such that the following conditions hold

1.

Hyperbolicity. For any
n\in\mathbb{N}
,
v^{u}
and
v^{s}
,

\displaystyle|D(f^{R})^{n}v^{u}|\geq C\beta^{-n}|v^{u}|,\quad|D(f^{R})^{n}v^{s}|\leq C\beta^{n}|v^{s}|,

where
|\cdot|
is the Riemannian metric induced from
\mathcal{M}
to (un)stable manifolds.

2.

SRB measures and u-SRB measures.
(X,f^{R},\mu_{X})
is a K-system where
\mu_{X}:=\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{M}}|_{X}}{\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(X)}
. A corresponding measurable K-partition consists of smooth pieces of stable manifolds. Moreover, conditional distributions on
\gamma^{u}
(say
\mu_{\gamma^{u}}
) are absolutely continuous w.r.t. Lebesgue measure
\Leb_{\gamma^{u}}
on
\gamma^{u}
.

3.

Distortion bounds. Let
d_{\gamma^{u}}(\cdot,\cdot)
be the distance measured along
\gamma^{u}
. By
\det D^{u}f^{R}
we denote the Jacobian of
Df^{R}
along the unstable manifolds. Then, if
x,y\in X
belong to a
\gamma^{u}
, such that
(f^{R})^{n}
is smooth on
\gamma^{u}
, the following relation holds

\displaystyle\log\frac{\det D^{u}(f^{R})^{n}(x)}{\det D^{u}(f^{R})^{n}(y)}\leq\psi\left[d_{\gamma^{u}}\Big((f^{R})^{n}x,(f^{R})^{n}y\Big)\right],

where
\psi(\cdot)
is some function, which does not depend on
\gamma^{u}
, and
\lim_{s\to 0^{+}}\psi(s)=0
.

4.

Bounded curvatures. The curvatures of all
\gamma^{u}
are uniformly
bounded by
C
.

5.

Absolute continuity. Consider a holonomy map
h:\gamma^{u}_{1}\to\gamma^{u}_{2}
, which maps a point
x\in\gamma^{u}_{1}
to the point
h(x)\in\gamma^{u}_{2}
, such that both
x
and
h(x)
belong to the same
\gamma^{s}
. We assume that the holonomy map satisfies the following relation

\displaystyle\frac{\det D^{u}(f^{R})^{n}(x)}{\det D^{u}(f^{R})^{n}\big(h(x)\big)}=C^{\pm 1}\text{ for all }n\geq 1\text{ and }x\in\gamma^{u}_{1},

6.

Growth lemmas. There exist
N\in\mathbb{N}
, sufficiently small
\delta_{0}>0
and
constants
\kappa,\sigma>0
which satisfy the following condition. For any
sufficiently small
\delta>0
and for any disk on a smooth unstable manifold
\gamma^{u}
with
\diam\gamma^{u}\leq\delta_{0}
, denote by
U_{\delta}\subseteq\gamma^{u}
a
\delta
-neighborhood of the subset
\gamma^{u}\bigcap\bigcup_{0\leq i\leq N}(f^{R})^{-i}\mathbb{S}
within the set
\gamma^{u}
. Then there exists an open subset
V_{\delta}\subseteq\gamma^{u}\setminus U_{\delta}
, such that
\Leb_{\gamma^{u}}\big(\gamma^{u}\setminus(U_{\delta}\bigcup V_{\delta})\big)=0
, and for any
\epsilon>0

\displaystyle\Leb_{\gamma^{u}}(r_{V_{\delta},N}<\epsilon)\leq 2\epsilon\beta+\epsilon C\delta_{0}^{-1}\Leb_{\gamma^{u}}(\gamma^{u}),

\displaystyle\Leb_{\gamma^{u}}(r_{U_{\delta},0}<\epsilon)\leq C\delta^{-\kappa}\epsilon,

\displaystyle\Leb_{\gamma^{u}}(U_{\delta})\leq C\delta^{\sigma},

where
r_{U_{\delta},0}(x):=d_{\gamma^{u}}(x,\partial U_{\delta})
,
r_{V_{\delta},N}(x):=d_{(f^{R})^{N}\gamma^{u}}\big((f^{R})^{N}x,\partial(f^{R})^{N}V_{\delta}(x)\big)
, and
V_{\delta}(x)
is a connected component of
V_{\delta}
, which contains
x
.

7.

Finiteness:
\int Rd\mu_{X}<\infty
.

8.

Mixing:
\gcd\{R\}=1
.

Remark 2
.

In the growth lemmas a positive integer
N
is usually chosen as a sufficiently large number. From the paper
Gurevič 1967
, the conditions that
\gcd{R}=1
and K-mixing of
f^{R}
guarantee that
f
is also K-mixing.

Consider now the first return tower

\displaystyle\Delta:=\{(x,n)\in X\times\{0,1,2,\cdots\}:n<R(x)\}.

A dynamics
F:\Delta\to\Delta
is defined as
F(x,n)=(x,n+1)
if
n+1\leq R(x)-1
and
F(x,n)=(f^{R}x,0)
if
n=R(x)-1
. The projection
\pi:\Delta\to\mathcal{M}
is defined by
\pi(x,n):=f^{n}(x)
as
\pi\circ F=f\circ\pi
. Finally we introduce projections
\pi_{X}:\Delta\to X
and
\pi_{\mathbb{N}}:\Delta\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
, so that for any
(x,n)\in\Delta

\pi_{X}(x,n)=x,\quad\pi_{\mathbb{N}}(x,n)=n.

(2.2)

Extend now
\mu_{X}
from
X
to
\Delta
as

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta}:=(\int Rd\mu_{X})^{-1}\sum_{j}(F^{j})_{*}\big(\mu_{X}|_{\{R>j\}}\big),

which reproduces the invariant probability measure on
\mathcal{M}

\displaystyle\mu_{\mathcal{M}}=(\pi)_{*}\mu_{\Delta}.

We identify
\Delta_{0}:=(X\times\{0\})\bigcap\Delta
with
X
,
\mu_{\Delta_{0}}
with
\mu_{X}
and
F^{R}
with
f^{R}
. Therefore
\pi:X\to X
is the identity map.

Note that
\pi:\Delta\to\mathcal{M}
is bijective. Thus
(\Delta,F)
is identical to
(\mathcal{M},f)
, and
(X,f^{R})=(\Delta_{0},F^{R})
is a CMZ structure of
(\Delta,F)
.

Remark 3
.

1.

If
R=1
, then
X=\mathcal{M}
, meaning that
(\mathcal{M},f,\mu_{\mathcal{M}})
has a CMZ structure.

2.

It follows from
Chernov 1999
,
Chernov and Zhang 2005
that
(X,f^{R},\mu_{X})
can be modelled by a hyperbolic Young tower
Young 1998
.

3.

It follows from
Chernov and Zhang 2005
,
Chernov and Zhang 2008
that the mixing rates for the dynamical system
(\mathcal{M},f,\mu_{\mathcal{M}})
are determined by the decay rate of
\mu_{X}(R>n)
. However, we do not use this fact in the present paper.

4.

(\mathcal{M},f,\mu_{\mathcal{M}})
is a K-system (and therefore mixing) because of the condition that
f^{R}
is K-mixing and
\gcd\{R\}=1
.

Definition 5

(Holes and dynamical point processes)
.

Throughout the paper, a hole within the boundary
\partial Q
is an open disk
B_{r}(q)
with radius
r
and the center at a regular point
q\in\partial Q
of the boundary of a billiard table.

We define now a dynamical point process
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}
on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}
. For any measurable
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}
, and any
x\in\mathcal{M}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(x)(A):

\displaystyle=\#\{i\geq 0:f^{i}(x)\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2],\quad i\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\in A\}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\in A}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}\circ f^{i}(x).

We will usually drop the symbol
x
and write

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(A)=\sum_{i\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\in A}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}\circ f^{i}.

By using
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}:=(\pi)_{*}\mu_{\Delta}
, we get

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(A)=\sum_{i\cdot\mu_{\Delta}(A_{r})\in A}\mathbbm{1}_{A_{r}}\circ F^{i},

where
A_{r}:=\pi^{-1}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])
.

Remark 4
.

Following the theory of point processes (see e.g. page 226 of
Kallenberg 2002
) we have that

\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(x)=\sum_{i\geq 0:f^{i}(x)\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}\delta_{i\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])},

where
\delta
is a Dirac measure. Hence,
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}
is a random counting measure, e.g.,
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(x)[0,1]
counts the number of
i\in[0,\frac{1}{\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\}}]
, such that
f^{i}(x)
lies in
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
.

Definition 6

(Poisson point processes)
.

We say that
\mathcal{P}
is a Poisson point process on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}
if

1.

\mathcal{P}
is a random counting measure on
\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}
.

2.

\mathcal{P}(A)
is a Poisson-distributed random variable for any Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}
.

3.

If
A_{1},A_{2},\cdots,A_{n}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}
are pairwise disjoint, then
\mathcal{P}(A_{1}),\cdots,\mathcal{P}(A_{n})
are independent.

4.

\mathbb{E}\mathcal{P}(A)=\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(A)
for any Borel set
A\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}
.

Definition 7

(Poisson approximations)
.

We say that
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}\to_{d}\mathcal{P}
if
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}f\to_{d}\mathcal{P}f
for any
f\in C^{+}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\int\exp{(-t\cdot\mathcal{N}^{r,q}f)}d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}=\int\exp{(-t\cdot\mathcal{P}f)}d\mathbb{P}\text{ for all }t>0,

where
C^{+}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\})
is the space consisting of positive continuous functions with a compact support, defined on
[0,\infty)
.

This is equivalent to the relation

\displaystyle(\mathcal{N}^{r,q}I_{1},\mathcal{N}^{r,q}I_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{N}^{r,q}I_{k})\to_{d}(\mathcal{P}I_{1},\mathcal{P}I_{2},\cdots,\mathcal{P}I_{k})

for any
k\in\mathbb{N}
and any bounded intervals
I_{1},\cdots I_{k}\subseteq{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}
. (See, e.g., Theorem 16.16 in
Kallenberg 2002
).

Thus, the limit distribution of
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}
is Poisson, when the disk
B_{r}(q)
shrinks to a regular point on the boundary
\partial Q
of a billiard table.

Definition 8

(Sections and quasi-sections)
.

Recall that
\pi_{X}:\Delta\to X
is defined as
\pi_{X}(x,n)=x
. We say that
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\subseteq\mathcal{M}
is a section if
\pi_{X}:\pi^{-1}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\to X
is injective for any sufficiently small
r>0
. Further,
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\subseteq\mathcal{M}
is a quasi-section if for any sufficiently small
r>0
there is a measurable set
S_{r}\subseteq B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, such that
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}[\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\setminus S_{r}]=o\big(\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big[B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big]\big)
, and
\pi_{X}:\pi^{-1}S_{r}\to X
is injective. In this case, we also refer to
S_{r}
as a section in
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
. We will explicitly write what it is for a given example throughout the present paper.

Remark 5
.

In the applications to two-dimensional billiards,
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a strip in
\mathcal{M}
, and
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}
is usually a union of finitely many rectangles, whose measures are of order
O(r^{2})
, see section
9
. To avoid unnecessary complications, we always assume that
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}
has a regular shape, e.g., as a union of finitely many rectangles.

Assumption 1

(
Geometric assumptions
)
.

1.

For a.e.
q\in\partial Q
the set
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section.

2.

\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\partial X_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{S}
(see the definition of
X_{i}
in (
2.1
)).

3.

There are constants
C>0
and
\alpha\in(0,1]
, such that for any
\gamma^{k}
,
k=u
or
s
(the condition
\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\partial X_{i}\subseteq\mathbb{S}
implies that
\gamma^{k}\subseteq X_{i}
for some
i\geq 1
), and for any
x,y\in\gamma^{k}
,

\displaystyle d_{f^{j}\gamma^{k}}(f^{j}x,f^{j}y)\leq Cd_{\gamma^{k}}(x,y)^{\alpha}\text{ for all }j\in[0,R(x)).

4.

There exist two cones
C^{u},C^{s}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{M})
, such that

\dim(\interior{C^{u}}\bigcap\interior{C^{s}})<1,\quad(Df)C^{u}\subseteq C^{u},\quad(Df)^{-1}C^{s}\subseteq C^{s},

and for all
n\geq 1
and
\Leb_{\partial Q}
-a.e.
q\in\partial Q

\dim\big(\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\bigcap\interior{C^{u}}\big)<1,\quad\dim\big(\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\bigcap\interior{C^{s}}\big)<1,

(Df)^{n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq{\interior{C^{u}}},\quad(Df)^{-n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq{\interior{C^{s}}},

where
\interior{C^{u}}
(resp.
\interior{C^{s}}
) is the interior of
C^{u}
(resp.
C^{s}
).

Remark 6
.

1.

We will present later an easy-to-implement scheme to verify the existence of
C^{u},C^{s}
for billiard systems. The cones
C^{u},C^{s}
should be transversal (but not necessarily uniformly transversal). This condition on
C^{u},C^{s}
in Assumption
1
is called an aperiodic condition, because it (almost surely) rules out the periodic orbits (see Lemma
22
).

2.

The Hölder condition is natural, and it is traditionally used for hyperbolic systems, and, particularly, for billiards.

Theorem 2.1

(Poisson limit laws)

Suppose that dynamical system
(\mathcal{M},f)
has a CMZ structure
(X,f^{R})
(see Definitions
4
and
2
), and the Assumption
1
holds. Then, when
r\to 0
, we have
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}\to_{d}\mathcal{P}
(see Definition
7
) for
\Leb_{\partial Q}
-a.s.
q\in\partial Q
.

Corollary 1

(First hitting)

Under the same conditions as in Theorem
2.1
consider the moment of time when the first hitting (passage) of the hole occurs, i.e.,
\tau_{r,q}(x):=\inf\left\{n\geq 1:f^{n}(x)\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\right\}
for any
x\in\mathcal{M}
. Then for any
t>0
and almost every
q\in\partial Q
, the following relation holds for the first hitting probability

\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big\{\tau_{r,q}>t\big/\mu\Big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\Big)\Big\}=e^{-t}.

Proof.

Clearly

\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\left\{\tau_{r,q}>t/\mu\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\right\}=\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\left\{\mathcal{N}^{r,q}\left[0,t/\mu\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\right]=0\right\},

Apply now Theorem
2.1
, and the corollary holds.
∎

2.2
Related work and comparison with our result

1.

Unlike
Haydn and Vaienti 2020
,
Pène and Saussol 2010
, Theorem
2.1
claims that validity of Poisson limit laws does not depend on the rate of correlations decay. In other words, decay of correlations can be arbitrarily slow as long as the first return time
R\in L^{1}
, i.e.,
R
must be just integrable.

2.

The technique used in
Pène and Saussol 2010
works only for Sinai billiards with finite horizons, while our approach works for arbitrarily slow mixing billiards, and particularly for Sinai billiards with infinite horizons.

3.

The papers
Pène and Saussol 2016
,
Pène and Saussol 2020
established the (spatio-temporal) Poisson limit law under several conditions including that contracting (resp. expanding) rates
\alpha
along stable (resp. unstable) manifolds and
\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\log\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\}}{\log r}
are sufficiently large, i.e.,
\alpha\cdot\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\log\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\}}{\log r}>1
. These conditions fail for billiards with focusing components of the boundary (e.g. if
\alpha=1
for stadium-type billiards).

4.

One of the main difficulties in proving Poisson limit laws, related to short returns, was outlined in
Pène and Saussol 2016
. The papers
Haydn and Vaienti 2020
,
Pène and Saussol 2020
,
Pène and Saussol 2010
,
Pène and Saussol 2016
handled it by inspecting the original dynamics
f
, which leads to a requirement of a fast mixing rate. Our approach via an inducing method allows to restrict a hole in the phase space to a good set, and it works for systems with arbitrarily slow mixing.

Another challenge for proving Poisson limit laws, called a corona, comes from the condition
\alpha\cdot\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\log\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\}}{\log r}>1
(see
Pène and Saussol 2016
). The failure of
\alpha\cdot\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\log\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\}}{\log r}>1
in this paper causes essential difficulties in proving Poisson limit laws. Our techniques, which combine the inducing method with an approximation method, allow to overcome this challenge.

5.

The papers
Pène and Saussol 2020
studied various holes in
\mathcal{M}
for Sinai billiards with bounded horizons and diamond billiards. Although in the present paper we consider a special type of holes, i.e., the ones in
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, our technique can be adapted for more general holes. The holes we consider here are the most natural for billiard systems and their applications. A consideration of a general type holes will make the paper much longer and even more technical.

6.

The method used in
Pène and Saussol 2020
requires, besides the existence of CMZ structure, some special properties of Sinai billiards with finite horizons and diamond billiards to hold, (e.g. see page 657 of
Pène and Saussol 2020
). Our method only uses the assumption of the existence of CMZ structure, and can be applied to a large class of billiards.

7.

Unlike
Bunimovich and Su 2022
, our result does not provide convergence rates to Poisson limit laws. We believe though that it is possible to get convergence rates by placing more restrictive conditions on the first return time
R
and singularities
\mathbb{S}
. We expect to deal with convergence rates in another paper.

2.3
An informal description of the scheme of proof of Theorem
2.1

1.

The lemmas in section
3
reduce the point process
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}
to a new point process generated by a section
S_{r}
in
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
.

2.

In section
4
Lemma
4
, we lift this new point process to
\Delta
, which becomes yet another new point process
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}
. Then we just need to prove a Poisson approximation for
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}
defined on
\Delta
.

3.

In section
4
we truncate the tower
\Delta
as
\Delta_{m}
with height
m
. Since
\lim_{m\to\infty}\Delta_{m}=\Delta
, we can restrict
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}
to
\Delta_{m}
, in order to investigate the Poisson approximation.

4.

For each
m\geq 1
, the truncated tower
\Delta_{m}
can “induce” a “better” hyperbolic system and a “better” point process
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
. Lemma
5
shows that, to study a Poisson approximation for
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}
, restricted to
\Delta_{m}
, one just needs to prove a Poisson approximation for
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
on
\Delta_{m}
.

5.

Lemma
6
shows that if, for any large
m\geq 1
, a Poisson approximation holds for
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
, which is defined on
\Delta_{m}
, then the Poisson approximation for
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}
in Theorem
2.1
holds. Since we study a specific large
m\geq 1
, a proof of Poisson approximations for
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
on
\Delta_{m}
does not require uniformity in
m\geq 1
.

6.

\Delta_{m}
can be modeled by a non-mixing hyperbolic Young tower as shown in Section
5
.

7.

The sections
6
,
7
and
8
apply the non-mixing hyperbolic Young tower of
\Delta_{m}
for proving a Poisson approximation for
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
for each large
m\geq 1
.

In section
6
, we obtain two conditions (
6.2
) and (
6.3
), which will be verified to prove Poisson approximations for
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
on
\Delta_{m}
.

The sections
7
and
8
deal with (
6.2
) and (
6.3
) respectively for
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
in the phase space. The idea is to compare a measure related to
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
with a measure of a family of hyperbolic product sets which have non-empty intersections with
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
.
The estimates in this procedure do not require uniformity in
m
. The Assumption
1
plays a crucial role in these estimates.

3
Poisson limit laws: from quasi-sections to sections

To prove Poisson limit laws (see Definition
7
), we will need one result from
Kallenberg 2002
. To simplify notations, we denote
\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}
by
\Leb
throughout this section.

Lemma 1

(See Proposition 16.17 of
Kallenberg 2002
)

For any
q\in\partial Q
convergence
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}\to_{d}\mathcal{P}
holds if

1.

For any compact set
K\subseteq[0,\infty)
,
\limsup_{r\to 0}\int\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(K)d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\Leb(K)
.

2.

For any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},J_{2},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big).

(3.1)

Now we will verify these conditions.

Lemma 2

For any compact set
K\subseteq[0,\infty)
,
\limsup_{r\to 0}\int\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(K)d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\Leb(K)
.

Proof.

Assume that
K\subseteq[0,T)
for sufficiently large
T>0
. Then
[0,T)\setminus K=\bigcup_{i\geq 1}(a_{i},b_{i})
for some disjoint open intervals
(a_{i},b_{i})
. Therefore,
K=\bigcap_{N\geq 1}\bigcap_{i\leq N}[0,T)\bigcap(a_{i},b_{i})^{c}
. Let
K_{N}:=\bigcap_{i\leq N}[0,T)\bigcap(a_{i},b_{i})^{c}
, which is a disjoint union of finitely many intervals
J_{1},J_{2},\cdots,J_{i_{N}}
. Hence

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(J_{i})d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle=\int\sum_{j\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\in J_{i}}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}\circ f^{j}d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\big[1+\Leb(J_{i})/\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\big]\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big).

Therefore

\displaystyle\int\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(K)d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\leq\int\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(K_{N})d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}=\int\sum_{j\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\in K_{N}}\mathbbm{1}_{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]}\circ f^{j}d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\leq i_{N}\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)+\Leb(K_{N}).

Thus
\limsup_{r\to 0}\int\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(K)d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\leq\Leb(K_{N})
. We conclude the proof by letting
N\to\infty
.
∎

Now we study the relation (
3.1
). Suppose that
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section for some small
r>0
. Hence there is a section
S_{r}\subseteq B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, such that
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}\big)=o\big(\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\big)
for any sufficiently small
r>0
. Define

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r,q}(A):=\sum_{i\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r})\in A}\mathbbm{1}_{S_{r}}\circ f^{i},

(3.2)

where
A
is a measurable set in
[0,\infty)
. Then we can further modify the relation (
3.1
) as follows.

Lemma 3

(From quasi-sections to sections)

For any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},J_{2},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big)=\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big).

Proof.

Denote
J^{\prime}_{i}:=\{x:x\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\in J_{i}\}
,
J^{\prime\prime}_{i}:=\{x:x\cdot\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r})\in J_{i}\}
. Now we estimate the difference between
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\big)
and
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\big)
.

\displaystyle\Big|\mu_{\mathcal{M}}

\displaystyle\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big)-\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\mathcal{N}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle=\Big|\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\Big)-\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\Big)-\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\Big)-\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\Big)\Big|.

Using
\big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\big)\setminus\big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)=\big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\big)\bigcap\big(\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\subseteq\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}\{f^{j}\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}\}
we can continue the estimate above as

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\Big)-\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup(J^{\prime}_{i}\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i})}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\Big)-\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup(J^{\prime}_{i}\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i})}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\Big)\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\bigcap\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\bigcap\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup(J^{\prime}_{i}\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i})}f^{j}\in S_{r}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\bigcap\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup(J^{\prime}_{i}\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i})}f^{j}\in S_{r}\Big)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}\Big).

(3.3)

There is a small
r_{J}>0
depending on
J_{1},J_{2},\cdots J_{n}
such that, for any
r\in(0,r_{J})
and all
i\leq n
, the interval
J^{\prime}_{i}
is the only possible one that intersect
J^{\prime\prime}_{i}
and
\Leb J^{\prime}_{i}\geq 1
,
\Leb J^{\prime\prime}_{i}\geq 1
. Therefore

\displaystyle\Big\{\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\bigcap\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup(J^{\prime}_{i}\bigcup J^{\prime\prime}_{i})}f^{j}\in S_{r}\Big\}\subseteq\Big\{\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup(J^{\prime}_{i}\setminus J^{\prime\prime}_{i})}f^{j}\in S_{r}\Big\},

\displaystyle\Big\{\bigcap_{j\in\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i}}f^{j}\notin S_{r}\bigcap\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup(J^{\prime\prime}_{i}\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i})}f^{j}\in S_{r}\Big\}\subseteq\Big\{\bigcup_{j\in\bigcup(J^{\prime\prime}_{i}\setminus J^{\prime}_{i})}f^{j}\in S_{r}\Big\},

and
J_{i}^{\prime}\setminus J_{i}^{\prime\prime},J_{i}^{\prime\prime}\setminus J_{i}^{\prime}
contain at most
\Leb(J_{i}^{\prime}\setminus J_{i}^{\prime\prime})+1,\Leb(J_{i}^{\prime\prime}\setminus J_{i}^{\prime})+1
positive integers, respectively.

For any
r\in(0,r_{J})
by making use of
f_{*}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}=\mu_{\mathcal{M}}
and the fact that
S_{r}
is a section in
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, we can continue the estimate (
3.3
) as

\displaystyle\leq\left[\Leb(\bigcup J_{i}^{\prime}\setminus J_{i}^{\prime\prime})+\Leb(\bigcup J_{i}^{\prime\prime}\setminus J_{i}^{\prime})+2n\right]\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r})+\Leb(\bigcup J^{\prime}_{i})\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}\Big)

\displaystyle\leq\left[2n\max_{i}J_{i}\left|\frac{1}{\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r})}-\frac{1}{\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])}\right|+2n\right]\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r})

\displaystyle\quad+n\max_{i}J_{i}\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}\Big)}{\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\Big)},

where
\max_{i}J_{i}
is the maximal positive number in
\bigcup_{i}J_{i}
. By letting
r\to 0
we conclude a proof of this lemma.
∎

It follows from Lemmas
1
,
2
, and
3
that, in order to prove Poisson approximations for quasi-sections
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
(see Theorem
2.1
), we just need to prove the following for sections
S_{r}\subseteq B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, i.e.,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big),

(3.4)

where
J_{1},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
are disjoint bounded intervals. The rest of the paper deals with a proof of the relation (
3.4
).

4
Inducing and approximations

To prove (
3.4
), we will give a sufficient condition in Lemma
6
for (
3.4
), which is deduced from the technical Lemmas
4
and
5
in this section.

Before proofs, we consider some notions and definitions used in this section for a CMZ structure (see Definition
4
).

Definition 9

(Truncated towers)
.

For each
m\geq 0
let a truncated sub-tower of
\Delta
be

\displaystyle\Delta_{m}:=\Delta\bigcap(X\times\{0,1,2,\cdots,m\}).

Define now projections
\pi_{X}:\Delta_{m}\to X
and
\pi_{\mathbb{N}}:\Delta_{m}\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
(we use here the same notations as in (
2.2
)) so that for any
(x,n)\in\Delta_{m}

\displaystyle\pi_{X}(x,n)=x,\quad\pi_{\mathbb{N}}(x,n)=n.

The first return time
R_{m}:\Delta_{m}\to\mathbb{N}
is

\displaystyle R_{m}(x)=\inf\{n\geq 1:F^{n}(x)\in\Delta_{m}\}\text{ for any }x\in\Delta_{m}.

Explicitly, for any
(x,l)\in\Delta_{m}
,

\displaystyle R_{m}(x,l)=\begin{cases}1,&l<\min\{R(x)-1,m\}\\
\max\{R(x)-m,1\},&l=\min\{R(x)-1,m\}\\
\end{cases}.

Thus we also have the first return map
F^{R_{m}}:\Delta_{m}\to\Delta_{m}
. Define the
i
-th return times
R_{m}^{i}
recursively as

\displaystyle R_{m}^{1}:=R_{m},\quad R_{m}^{i}:=R_{m}^{i-1}+R_{m}\circ F^{R_{m}}\text{ for any }i\geq 2.

A probability distribution on
\Delta_{m}
is defined by

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{m}}:=(\int\min\{R,m+1\}d\mu_{X})^{-1}\sum_{j\leq m}(F^{j})_{*}\big(\mu_{X}|_{\{R>j\}}\big).

Note that the relation
\int R_{m}d\mu_{\Delta_{m}}=\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}
follows from the Kac’s lemma (see
Kac 1947
).

Now, a map
\pi_{\Delta_{m}}:\Delta\to\Delta_{m}
is defined so, that for any
(x,n)\in\Delta
,

\displaystyle\pi_{\Delta_{m}}(x,n)=\begin{cases}(x,n),&n\leq m\\
(x,m),&n>m\\
\end{cases},

i.e.,
\pi_{\Delta_{m}}
pulls every element of
\Delta\setminus\Delta_{m}
back to the roof of
\Delta_{m}
and keeps the elements in
\Delta_{m}
unchanged.

Observe also that, if
R(x)>m
for
x\in X
, then
F^{R^{m+1}_{m}}(x)=F^{R}(x)=f^{R}(x)
.

Remark 7
.

Since
S_{r}
is a section, then
\pi_{X}:\pi^{-1}S_{r}\to X
is injective, and thus
\pi_{X}:\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}\to X
is also injective.

Now we introduce some point processes for the section
S_{r}
, which is contained in the quasi-section
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\subseteq\mathcal{M}
.

Definition 10
.

For each
m\geq 1
, define point processes on
(\Delta_{m},\mu_{\Delta_{m}})
so that for any measurable set
A\subseteq[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(A):=\sum_{i\cdot\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})\in A}\mathbbm{1}_{\pi^{-1}S_{r}}\circ F^{i},

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}(A):=\sum_{k\cdot\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})\in A}\mathbbm{1}_{\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{k}.

Note that
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}
can be viewed as a point process on
(\Delta,\mu_{\Delta})
. Using
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}=_{d}\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r,q}
, where the last one was defined in (
3.2
), we get the following lemma.

Lemma 4

(Lifting)

Suppose that for any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big).

Then for any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{i\leq n}J_{i})=0\Big).

For each
m\geq 1
, we can now study a limit law for
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
, which is defined on
\Delta_{m}
.

Lemma 5

(Inducing)

For
m\geq 1
, suppose that for any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big).

Then for any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big).

Proof.

We divide the proof into several steps.

As the first step, we introduce hitting times and their properties. For any
x\in\Delta_{m}
define

\displaystyle\tau^{1}_{s}(x):=\inf\{n\geq 1:F^{n}(x)\in\pi^{-1}S_{r}\},

\displaystyle\tau^{1}_{p}(x):=\inf\{n\geq 1:F^{n}(x)\in\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}\},

\displaystyle\tau^{1}_{i}(x):=\inf\{n\geq 1:(F^{R_{m}})^{n}(x)\in\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}\}.

The corresponding
j
-th (
j\geq 2
) return times
\tau_{s}^{j},\tau_{p}^{j},\tau_{i}^{j}
are defined inductively as follows

\displaystyle\tau^{j}_{s}(x):=\inf\{n>\tau^{j-1}_{s}(x):F^{n}(x)\in\pi^{-1}S_{r}\},

\displaystyle\tau^{j}_{p}(x):=\inf\{n>\tau^{j-1}_{p}(x):F^{n}(x)\in\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}\},

\displaystyle\tau^{j}_{i}(x):=\inf\{n>\tau^{j-1}_{i}(x):(F^{R_{m}})^{n}(x)\in\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}\}.

So for any
j\geq 1
we have

\displaystyle\tau_{s}:=\tau_{s}^{1}<\tau_{s}^{2}<\cdots<\tau_{s}^{j}<\tau_{s}^{j+1}<\cdots\to\infty,

\displaystyle\tau_{p}:=\tau_{p}^{1}<\tau_{p}^{2}<\cdots<\tau_{p}^{j}<\tau_{p}^{j+1}<\cdots\to\infty,

\displaystyle\tau_{i}:=\tau_{i}^{1}<\tau_{i}^{2}<\cdots<\tau_{i}^{j}<\tau_{i}^{j+1}<\cdots\to\infty.

By making use of the fact that
S_{r}
is a section and Remark
7
, we get that for any
j\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\tau^{j}_{p}=R_{m}+R_{m}\circ F^{R_{m}}+\cdots+R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{\tau^{j}_{i}-1},

\displaystyle|\tau^{j}_{p}-\tau^{j}_{s}|\leq R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{\tau^{j}_{i}}.

From the Birkhoff’s ergodic theorem we obtain

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{i}}{n}=\int R_{m}d\mu_{\Delta_{m}}=\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}\quad\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\text{-a.s.}

\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{n}}{n}=0\quad\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\text{-a.s.}

Therefore, for any sufficiently small
\epsilon^{\prime}\in(0,\min\{\frac{1}{2\mu_{\Delta}(X)},\min_{j}\diam J_{j}\})
and a.e.
x\in\Delta_{m}
, there is
N_{\epsilon^{\prime},x}>0
, such that for any
n>N_{\epsilon^{\prime},x}
,

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{i}}{n}-\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}\Big|\leq\epsilon^{\prime},\quad\Big|\frac{R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{n}}{n}\Big|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

Let
G_{n,\epsilon^{\prime}}:=\{x\in\Delta_{m}:n\geq N_{\epsilon^{\prime},x}\}
. It is obvious that
G_{n,\epsilon^{\prime}}\subseteq G_{n+1,\epsilon^{\prime}}
for any
n\geq 1
. Hence
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(G_{n,\epsilon^{\prime}})=1
. Therefore, there is
N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}>0
, such that for any
n\geq N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
,
\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(G_{n,\epsilon^{\prime}}^{c})\leq\epsilon^{\prime}
.

Let
x\in G_{N_{\epsilon^{\prime}},\epsilon^{\prime}}
. We have that
x\in G_{n,\epsilon^{\prime}}
for any
n\geq N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
and

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\sum_{i=0}^{n-1}R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{i}}{n}-\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}\Big|\leq\epsilon^{\prime},\quad\Big|\frac{R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{n}}{n}\Big|\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

In particular, if
\tau_{i}\geq N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
, (which implies
\tau_{i}^{j}\geq N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}
for all
j\geq 1
), then for all
j\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{p}^{j}}{\tau_{i}^{j}}\in[\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}-\epsilon^{\prime},\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}+\epsilon^{\prime}],

\displaystyle\Big|\frac{\tau_{s}^{j}}{\tau_{i}^{j}}-\frac{\tau_{p}^{j}}{\tau_{i}^{j}}\Big|\leq\frac{R_{m}\circ(F^{R_{m}})^{\tau_{i}^{j}}}{\tau_{i}^{j}}\leq\epsilon^{\prime}.

Therefore, for any
j\geq 1

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{s}^{j}}{\tau_{i}^{j}}\in[\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}-2\epsilon^{\prime},\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}+2\epsilon^{\prime}].

Now let
H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}:=\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\tau_{i}(x)>N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\}
. Then

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{c})=\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\tau_{i}(x)\leq N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\}

\displaystyle\leq N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle=N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\frac{\int Rd\mu_{X}}{\int\min\{R,m+1\}d\mu_{X}}\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle=N_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\frac{\int Rd\mu_{X}}{\int\min\{R,m+1\}d\mu_{X}}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r}),

where the first equality holds because
S_{r}
is a section.

Therefore, there is
r_{\epsilon^{\prime}}>0
, such that for any
r\in(0,r_{\epsilon^{\prime}})
,

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{c})\leq\epsilon^{\prime},\quad\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}^{c}\bigcup G_{N_{\epsilon^{\prime}},\epsilon^{\prime}}^{c})\leq 2\epsilon^{\prime}.

(4.1)

Besides, for any
x\in H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigcap G_{N_{\epsilon^{\prime}},\epsilon^{\prime}}
, and any
j\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\frac{\tau_{s}^{j}(x)}{\tau_{i}^{j}(x)}\in[\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}-2\epsilon^{\prime},\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}+2\epsilon^{\prime}].

(4.2)

As the second step, we connect
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
with the hitting times
\tau_{s}^{j},\tau_{i}^{j}
.

Let
aJ_{k}:=\{aj:j\in J_{k}\}
for any
a>0
, and
J_{k}^{\prime}:=\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k}
,

\displaystyle J_{k}^{\prime\prime}:

\displaystyle=(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}-2\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}J_{k}^{\prime}\bigcap(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}+2\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}J_{k}^{\prime}

(4.3)

\displaystyle=(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}-2\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k}\bigcap(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}+2\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k}

\displaystyle=(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}-2\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\mu_{\Delta}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k}\bigcap(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}+2\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\mu_{\Delta}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k}

\displaystyle=(1-2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k}\bigcap(1+2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k},

where the third equality holds because
S_{r}
is a section. Next, let

\displaystyle J_{k}^{\prime\prime\prime}:=(1-2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}J_{k}\bigcap(1+2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}J_{k},

which implies that
\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k}^{\prime\prime\prime}=J_{k}^{\prime\prime}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}=\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\tau^{j}_{s}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}J^{\prime}_{k}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\},

(4.4)

\displaystyle\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k}^{\prime\prime\prime})(x)=0\}=\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\tau^{j}_{i}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}J^{\prime\prime}_{k}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}.

(4.5)

As the third step, we estimate
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}
via
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
by applying (
4.1
) and (
4.4
),

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{m}}

\displaystyle\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\tau^{j}_{s}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}J^{\prime}_{k}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigcap G_{N_{\epsilon^{\prime}},\epsilon^{\prime}}:\tau^{j}_{s}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}J^{\prime}_{k}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}).

By using (
4.2
) and (
4.3
), the expression above can be estimated as

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigcap G_{N_{\epsilon^{\prime}},\epsilon^{\prime}}:\tau^{j}_{i}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}J^{\prime\prime}_{k}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\tau^{j}_{i}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k}^{\prime\prime}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\tau^{j}_{i}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k}^{\prime\prime}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+2\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}).

Now, according to (
4.5
), the inequality above can be written as

\displaystyle=\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k}^{\prime\prime\prime})(x)=0\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+2\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k}^{\prime\prime\prime})(x)=0\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+\frac{2\int Rd\mu_{X}}{\int\min\{R,m+1\}d\mu_{X}}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r}).

From the conditions of this lemma we have

\displaystyle\limsup_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}\leq e^{-\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k}^{\prime\prime\prime})}+2\epsilon^{\prime}.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}\to 0
. Then
\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k}^{\prime\prime\prime})\to\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})
and

\displaystyle\limsup_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}\leq e^{-\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})}.

As the fourth step, we connect
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r},\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
with the hitting times
\tau_{s}^{j},\tau_{i}^{j}
in a different way.

Let
\hat{J_{k}}:=(1-2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}J_{k}\bigcup(1+2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})^{-1}J_{k}
,
\hat{J_{k}^{\prime}}:=\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}\hat{J_{k}}
,

\displaystyle\hat{J_{k}^{\prime\prime}}:

\displaystyle=(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}-2\epsilon^{\prime})\hat{J_{k}^{\prime}}\bigcap(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}+2\epsilon^{\prime})\hat{J_{k}^{\prime}}

(4.6)

\displaystyle=(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}-2\epsilon^{\prime})\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}\hat{J_{k}}\bigcap(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})^{-1}+2\epsilon^{\prime})\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}\hat{J_{k}}

\displaystyle=(1-2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})\mu_{\Delta}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}\hat{J_{k}}\bigcap(1+2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})\mu_{\Delta}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}\hat{J_{k}}

\displaystyle=(1-2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}\hat{J_{k}}\bigcap(1+2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}\hat{J_{k}},,

where the fourth equality holds because
S_{r}
is a section. Clearly,

\displaystyle J_{k}=(1-2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})\hat{J_{k}}\bigcap(1+2\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\epsilon^{\prime})\hat{J_{k}},\quad\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1}J_{k}=\hat{J_{k}^{\prime\prime}}.

Then we have

\displaystyle\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}})(x)=0\}=\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\tau^{j}_{i}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}^{\prime}}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\},

(4.7)

\displaystyle\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}=\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\tau^{j}_{s}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}.

(4.8)

As the fifth step, we estimate
\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}
via
\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}
from (
4.1
) and (
4.7
)

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{m}}

\displaystyle\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}})(x)=0\}

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\tau^{j}_{i}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}^{\prime}}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigcap G_{N_{\epsilon^{\prime}},\epsilon^{\prime}}:\tau^{j}_{i}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}^{\prime}}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}).

By using (
4.2
) and (
4.6
) the inequality above can be written as

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in H_{\epsilon^{\prime}}\bigcap G_{N_{\epsilon^{\prime}},\epsilon^{\prime}}:\tau^{j}_{s}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\tau^{j}_{s}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\tau^{j}_{s}(x)\notin\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}^{\prime\prime}}\text{ for all }j\geq 1\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+2\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}).

Now, from (
4.8
) the inequality above can be estimated as

\displaystyle=\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}\setminus\pi^{-1}S_{r}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+2\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}+2\epsilon^{\prime}+\frac{2\int Rd\mu_{X}}{\int\min\{R,m+1\}d\mu_{X}}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r}).

From the conditions of this lemma we have

\displaystyle\liminf_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}\geq e^{-\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J_{k}})}-2\epsilon^{\prime}.

Let
\epsilon^{\prime}\to 0
. Then
\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}\hat{J}_{k})\to\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})
and

\displaystyle\liminf_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}\geq e^{-\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})}.

The previous steps lead to the following conclusion

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\{x\in\Delta_{m}:\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})(x)=0\}=e^{-\Leb_{\mathbb{R}^{+}\bigcup\{0\}}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})}=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)

for any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
. Hence, this lemma is proved.
∎

Remark 8
.

In
Bruin et al. 2003
a similar result was obtained for return statistics to balls. We are dealing here with a more general hitting statistics for sections.

Together with Lemmas
4
and
5
, the last lemma (an approximation technique) in this section provides a sufficient condition for (
3.4
).

Lemma 6

(Approximations)

Suppose that for any sufficiently large
m\geq 1
, and any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{i}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big).

(4.9)

Then for any disjoint bounded intervals
J_{1},\cdots,J_{n}\subseteq[0,\infty)
we have

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big).

By Lemma
4
we also get the required relation (
3.4
),

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r,q}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big),

provided that (
4.9
) holds for each large
m\geq 1
. Then this concludes a proof of Poisson limit laws in Theorem
2.1
.

Proof.

It follows from Lemma
5
that for any
m\gg 1

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big).

Therefore,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)+O\Big(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m}^{c})\Big)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m})\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)+O\Big(\mu_{\Delta}(\Delta_{m}^{c})\Big).

Let
m\to\infty
, then
\lim_{r\to 0}\mu_{\Delta}\Big(\mathcal{N}_{s}^{r}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)=\mathbb{P}\Big(\mathcal{P}(\bigcup_{k\leq n}J_{k})=0\Big)
.
∎

Remark 9
.

Thanks to Lemmas
6
,
3
,
2
and
1
, in order to prove that
\mathcal{N}^{r,q}\to_{d}\mathcal{P}
in Theorem
2.1
, we just need to verify (
4.9
) for the dynamical system
(\Delta_{m},F^{R_{m}},\mu_{\Delta_{m}})
for each large
m\geq 1
. Indeed,
(\Delta_{m},F^{R_{m}},\mu_{\Delta_{m}})
is a hyperbolic dynamical system with exponential decay of correlations and arbitrarily large contracting (resp. expanding) rates
\alpha
along stable (resp. unstable) manifolds. It allows us to skip verification of the condition
\alpha\cdot\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\log\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\{B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\}}{\log r}>1
(see
Pène and Saussol 2016
,
Pène and Saussol 2020
), which fails for many slowly mixing billiard systems.

5
Thicker hyperbolic and expanding Young towers

In order to prove (
4.9
), we will model dynamical systems
(\Delta_{m},F^{R_{m}},\mu_{\Delta_{m}})
by hyperbolic (although non-mixing) Young towers (see
Young 1998
,
Young 1999
), but with exponential contracting (resp. expanding) rates along stable (resp. unstable) manifolds. Some properties of non-mixing hyperbolic Young towers are introduced in this section.

5.1
Hyperbolic Young towers for
(X,f^{R},\mu_{X})

In this subsection we will consider a dynamical system
(X,f^{R},\mu_{X})
. To simplify notations denote
f^{R}
or
F^{R}
by
g
. According to
Chernov 1999
,
Chernov and Zhang 2005
,
Markarian 2004
and Definition
4
(CMZ structures), there exists a compact set
\Lambda\subseteq\bigcap_{0\leq i\leq N}g^{i}\mathbb{S}^{c}\bigcap\bigcap_{0\leq i\leq N}g^{-i}\mathbb{S}^{c}\subseteq X
with a hyperbolic product structure (here
N
is defined in the Definition
4
). Besides, there are families of
C^{1}
-stable disks (i.e., closed connected pieces of stable manifolds)
\Gamma^{s}:=\{\gamma^{s}\}
, and families of
C^{1}
-unstable disks (i.e., closed connected pieces of unstable manifolds)
\Gamma^{u}:=\{\gamma^{u}\}
, such that the following conditions hold.

1.

\Lambda=\left(\bigcup\gamma^{s}\right)\bigcap\left(\bigcup\gamma^{u}\right)
,

2.

\dim\gamma^{s}+\dim\gamma^{u}=\dim\mathcal{M}
,

3.

each
\gamma^{s}
intersects every
\gamma^{u}

at exactly one point,

4.

stable and unstable manifolds are transversal, and the angles between them are uniformly bounded away from 0,

5.

\Gamma^{u}:=\{\gamma^{u}\}
is a continuous family, i.e., there is a compact set
K^{s}
, a unit disk
O^{u}
in some Euclidean space and a map
\phi^{u}:K^{s}\times O^{u}\to\mathcal{M}
, such that

(a)

\gamma^{u}=\phi^{u}\left(\{x\}\times O^{u}\right)
is an unstable manifold,

(b)

\phi^{u}
maps
K^{s}\times O^{u}
homeomorphically onto its image,

(c)

x\to\phi^{u}|_{\{x\}\times O^{u}}
defines a continuous map from
K^{s}
to
\Emb^{1}\left(O^{u},\mathcal{M}\right)
, where
\Emb^{1}\left(O^{u},\mathcal{M}\right)
is the space of
C^{1}
-embeddings of
O^{u}
into
\mathcal{M}
.

\Gamma^{s}:=\{\gamma^{s}\}
is also a continuous family in the same sense.

6.

Lebesgue detectability: there exists
\gamma\in\Gamma^{u}
such that
\Leb_{\gamma}\left(\Lambda\bigcap\gamma\right)>0
, where
\Leb_{\gamma}
is the Lebesgue measure on
\gamma
.

7.

Markov property: there exist pairwise disjoint
s
-subsets
\Lambda_{1},\Lambda_{2},\cdots\subseteq\Lambda
, such that , each
\Lambda_{i}=\left(\bigcup_{\gamma^{s}\in\Gamma^{s}_{i}}\gamma^{s}\right)\bigcap\left(\bigcup_{\gamma^{u}\in\Gamma^{u}}\gamma^{u}\right)
for some
\Gamma^{s}_{i}\subseteq\Gamma^{s}
, and

(a)

\Leb_{\gamma}\left(\Lambda\setminus(\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\Lambda_{i})\right)=0
on each
\gamma\in\Gamma^{u}
,

(b)

there is a return time function
R^{e}:\Lambda\to\mathbb{N}
and a return map
g^{R^{e}}:\Lambda\to\Lambda
, such that for each
i\geq 1

R^{e}\big|_{\Lambda_{i}}:=R^{e}_{i}\equiv 0\pmod{N},\quad g^{R^{e}}\big|_{\Lambda_{i}}:=g^{R^{e}_{i}}\big|_{\Lambda_{i}},

g^{R^{e}_{i}}(\Lambda_{i})
is a
u
-subset (i.e., each
g^{R^{e}_{i}}(\Lambda_{i})=\left(\bigcup_{\gamma^{s}\in\Gamma^{s}}\gamma^{s}\right)\bigcap\left(\bigcup_{\gamma^{u}\in\Gamma^{u}_{i}}\gamma^{u}\right)
for some
\Gamma^{u}_{i}\subseteq\Gamma^{u}
), and for all
x\in\Lambda_{i}
,

\displaystyle g^{R^{e}_{i}}\Big(\gamma^{s}(x)\Big)\subseteq\gamma^{s}\Big(g^{R^{e}_{i}}(x)\Big),\quad g^{R^{e}_{i}}\Big(\gamma^{u}(x)\Big)\supseteq\gamma^{u}\Big(g^{R^{e}_{i}}(x)\Big),

where
\gamma^{u}(y)
(resp.
\gamma^{s}(y)
) is an element of
\Gamma^{u}
(resp.
\Gamma^{s}
)
which contains
y\in\Lambda
, and
N
is defined in
4
.

Moreover, there exist constants
C\geq 1
and
0<\beta<1
such that the following conditions hold.

8.

Exponential contraction of stable disks: for any
\gamma^{s}\in\Gamma^{s},x,y\in\gamma^{s},n\geq 1
,

d\Big(g^{n}(x),g^{n}(y)\Big)\leq C\beta^{n}.

9.

Backward exponential contraction of unstable disks: for any
\gamma^{u}\in\Gamma^{u},x,y\in\gamma^{u},n\geq 1
,

d\Big(g^{-n}(x),g^{-n}(y)\Big)\leq C\beta^{n}.

10.

Bounded distortion: for any
\gamma\in\Gamma^{u}\text{ and }x,y\in\gamma\bigcap\Lambda_{i}
for some
\Lambda_{i}
,

\log\frac{\det(D^{u}g^{R^{e}})(x)}{\det(D^{u}g^{R^{e}})(y)}\leq C\beta^{s\left(g^{R^{e}}(x),g^{R^{e}}(y)\right)},

where
s(x,y)
is the separation time, i.e., for any
x,y\in\Lambda
,

s(x,y):=\min\{n\geq 0:(g^{R^{e}})^{n}(x)\text{ and }(g^{R^{e}})^{n}(y)\text{ belong to different sets }\Lambda_{i}\}.

11.

Regularity of the stable foliations: for each
\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}\in\Gamma^{u}
, define
\Theta_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}:\gamma^{\prime}\bigcap\Lambda\to\gamma\bigcap\Lambda
by

\Theta_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}(x)=\gamma^{s}(x)\bigcap\gamma.

Then the following properties hold

(a)

\Theta_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}
is absolutely continuous, and for any
x\in\gamma\bigcap\Lambda

\frac{d\left(\Theta_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}\right)_{*}\Leb_{\gamma^{\prime}}}{d\Leb_{\gamma}}(x)=\prod_{n\geq 0}\frac{\det(D^{u}g)\left(g^{n}\left(x\right)\right)}{\det(D^{u}g)\big(g^{n}(\Theta_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}^{-1}x)\big)}=C^{\pm 1},

(b)

for any
x,y\in\gamma\bigcap\Lambda
,

\log\frac{\frac{d\left(\Theta_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}\right)_{*}\Leb_{\gamma^{\prime}}}{d\Leb_{\gamma}}(x)}{\frac{d\left(\Theta_{\gamma,\gamma^{\prime}}\right)_{*}\Leb_{\gamma^{\prime}}}{d\Leb_{\gamma}}(y)}\leq C\beta^{s(x,y)}.

12.

Decay rate of the return times
R^{e}
: for any
\gamma\in\Gamma^{u}

\displaystyle\Leb_{\gamma}\left(R^{e}>n\right)\leq C\beta^{n}.

(5.1)

Remark 10
.

Note that
\gcd{\{R^{e}\}}\geq N
. Indeed, it follows from the fact that
(f^{R})^{N}
satisfies the growth lemmas for a CMZ structure.

Now we can construct a hyperbolic Young tower
\Delta_{e}
with dynamics
F_{e}:\Delta_{e}\to\Delta_{e}
, where

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}:=\{(x,l)\in\Lambda\times\mathbb{N}_{0}:0\leq l<R^{e}(x)\},

\displaystyle F_{e}(x,l):=\begin{cases}(x,l+1),&l<R^{e}(x)-1\\
\left(g^{R^{e}}(x),0\right),&l=R^{e}(x)-1\\
\end{cases},

and define a projection
\pi_{e}:\Delta_{e}\to X
such that

\pi_{e}(x,l):=g^{l}(x).

The equivalence relation
\sim
on
\Lambda
is then

x\sim y\text{ if and only if }x,y\in\gamma^{s}\text{ for some }\gamma^{s}\in\Gamma^{s}.

Another equivalence relation
\sim
on
\Delta_{e}
is

(x,n)\sim(y,m)\text{ if and only if }x,y\in\gamma^{s}\text{ for some }\gamma^{s}\in\Gamma^{s},n=m.

By making use of these equivalence relations we can define a quotient tower
\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}:=\Delta_{e}/\sim
, which has a quotient product structure
\widetilde{\Lambda}:=\Lambda/\sim
with canonical projections
\widetilde{\pi_{\Delta_{e}}}:\Delta_{e}\to\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}
and
\widetilde{\pi_{\Lambda}}:\Lambda\to\widetilde{\Lambda}
. We identify
\Lambda,\widetilde{\Lambda}
with
\Delta_{e}\bigcap(\Lambda\times\{0\}),\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}\bigcap(\widetilde{\Lambda}\times\{0\})
, respectively. The quotient maps
\widetilde{F_{e}}:\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}\to\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}
,
\widetilde{g^{R^{e}}}:\widetilde{\Lambda}\to\widetilde{\Lambda}
, a quotient return time
\widetilde{R^{e}}:\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}\to\mathbb{N}
, and a quotient separation time
\widetilde{s}
on
\widetilde{\Lambda}\times\widetilde{\Lambda}
are defined via the following relations

\widetilde{\pi_{\Delta_{e}}}\circ F_{e}=\widetilde{F_{e}}\circ\widetilde{\pi_{\Delta_{e}}},\quad R^{e}=\widetilde{R^{e}}\circ\widetilde{\pi_{\Lambda}},\quad\widetilde{g^{R^{e}}}=\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}},\quad s=\widetilde{s}\circ(\widetilde{\pi_{\Lambda}},\widetilde{\pi_{\Lambda}}).

It follows from
Young 1998
that there exists a measure
m
on
\widetilde{\Lambda}
such that for any
x,y\in\widetilde{\Lambda}
with
\widetilde{s}(x,y)\geq 1
,

\displaystyle\log\frac{\det D\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}}(x)}{\det D\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}}(y)}\leq C\beta^{\widetilde{s}(\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}}x,\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}}y)},

(5.2)

where
\det D\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}}
is the Radon-Nikodym derivative of
\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}}
with respect to the measure
m
.

The set
(\widetilde{\Delta_{e}},\widetilde{F_{e}})
, together with (
5.2
), is called an expanding quotient Young tower.

Lemma 7

(See
Young 1998
,
Young 1999
)

There are constants
C\geq 1
and
0<\beta<1
, such that the following holds.

1.

There exists a probability distribution
\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}
on
\widetilde{\Lambda}
, which is constructed only from the measure
m
and
\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}}
, such that

\displaystyle(\widetilde{F_{e}}^{\widetilde{R^{e}}})_{*}\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}=\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}},\quad\frac{d\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}}{dm}=C^{\pm 1}.

(5.3)

A probability distribution on
\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}
, defined by

\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}}:=(\int\widetilde{R^{e}}d\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}})^{-1}\sum_{j}(\widetilde{F_{e}}^{j})_{*}\big(\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}|_{\{\widetilde{R^{e}}>j\}}\big),

is an invariant measure, i.e.,

\displaystyle\widetilde{F_{e}}_{*}\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}}=\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}}.

2.

Further, there exists a probability measure
\mu_{\Lambda}
on
\Lambda
such that

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{\pi_{\Lambda}}\right)_{*}\mu_{\Lambda}=\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}},\quad(g^{R^{e}})_{*}\mu_{\Lambda}=\mu_{\Lambda},\quad(\mu_{\Lambda})_{\gamma^{u}}\ll\Leb_{\gamma^{u}},\quad\frac{d(\mu_{\Lambda})_{\gamma^{u}}}{d\Leb_{\gamma^{u}}}=C^{\pm 1},

(5.4)

where
(\mu_{\Lambda})_{\gamma^{u}}
is the conditional probability of
\mu_{\Lambda}
on
\gamma^{u}\in\Gamma^{u}
. A probability measure on
\Delta_{e}
defined by

\mu_{\Delta_{e}}:=(\int R^{e}d\mu_{\Lambda})^{-1}\sum_{j}(F_{e}^{j})_{*}\big(\mu_{\Lambda}|_{\{R^{e}>j\}}\big)

has the following properties

\displaystyle\left(\widetilde{\pi_{\Delta_{e}}}\right)_{*}\mu_{\Delta_{e}}=\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}},\quad{\pi_{e}}_{*}\mu_{\Delta_{e}}=\mu_{X},\quad{F_{e}}_{*}\mu_{\Delta_{e}}=\mu_{\Delta_{e}}.

3.

Suppose that
\gcd{\{R^{e}\}}=N_{e}\geq N
. Now we define new towers

\displaystyle\Delta_{e}^{\prime}:=\{(x,lN_{e})\in\Lambda\times\mathbb{N}_{0}:0\leq l<R^{e}(x)/N_{e}\},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}:=\{(x,lN_{e})\in\widetilde{\Lambda}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}:0\leq l<\widetilde{R^{e}}(x)/N_{e}\},

which are sub-towers of
\Delta_{e}
and
\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}
, respectively. Then the maps
F_{e}^{N_{e}}:\Delta_{e}^{\prime}\to\Delta_{e}^{\prime}
and
\widetilde{F_{e}}^{N_{e}}:\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}\to\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}
preserve probability measures

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}:=(\int R^{e}/N_{e}d\mu_{\Lambda})^{-1}\sum_{j}(F_{e}^{jN_{e}})_{*}\big(\mu_{\Lambda}|_{\{R^{e}/N_{e}>j\}}\big),

\displaystyle\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}}:=(\int\widetilde{R^{e}}/N_{e}d\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}})^{-1}\sum_{j}(\widetilde{F_{e}}^{jN_{e}})_{*}\big(\mu_{\widetilde{\Lambda}}|_{\{\widetilde{R^{e}}/N_{e}>j\}}\big),

respectively. Further,
{\pi_{e}}_{*}\mu_{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}
is exactly
\mu_{X}
, since
(X,g,\mu_{X})
is mixing (see Definition
4
).

4.

A family of partitions
(\mathcal{Q}_{k})_{k\geq 0}
of
\Delta_{e}^{\prime}
,
(\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}_{k}})_{k\geq 0}
of
\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}
, defined as

\displaystyle\mathcal{Q}_{0}:=\{\Lambda_{i}\times\{lN_{e}\},i\geq 1,0\leq l<R^{e}_{i}/N_{e}\},\quad\mathcal{Q}_{k}:=\bigvee_{0\leq i\leq k}(F_{e}^{N_{e}})^{-i}\mathcal{Q}_{0},

\displaystyle\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}_{0}}:=\{\widetilde{\Lambda_{i}}\times\{lN_{e}\},i\geq 1,0\leq l<R^{e}_{i}/N_{e}\},\quad\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}:=\bigvee_{0\leq i\leq k}(\widetilde{F_{e}}^{N_{e}})^{-i}\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}_{0}},

satisfies the relations

\displaystyle\diam\left(\pi_{e}\circ F_{e}^{N_{e}k}(Q)\right)\leq C\beta^{kN_{e}},\quad\widetilde{\pi_{\Delta_{e}}}Q\in\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}_{2k}}\text{ for any }Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2k}.

5.

Finally, for any
n>2k\geq 2
,any
(\widetilde{Q_{i}})_{i\geq 1}\subseteq\widetilde{\mathcal{Q}_{k}}
, and any bounded function
\widetilde{h}:\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}\to\mathbb{R}
, we have the following estimate of decay of correlations

\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\widetilde{Q_{i}}}\widetilde{h}\circ(\widetilde{F_{e}}^{N_{e}})^{n}d\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}}-\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}}\Big(\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\widetilde{Q_{i}}\Big)\int\widetilde{h}d\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}}\Big|\leq C\beta^{n-2k}\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}}\Big(\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\widetilde{Q_{i}}\Big)||\widetilde{h}||_{\infty}.

(5.5)

From (
5.5
) and
\left(\widetilde{\pi_{\Delta_{e}}}\right)_{*}\mu_{\Delta_{e}}=\mu_{\widetilde{\Delta_{e}}}
, immediately follows an estimate of a rate of decay of correlations. Namely, for any
n>2k\geq 2
, any
(Q_{i})_{i\geq 1}\subseteq\mathcal{Q}_{k}
, and any
\sigma(\bigcup_{k\geq 0}\mathcal{Q}_{k})
-measurable function
h:\Delta_{e}^{\prime}\to\mathbb{R}
,

\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{i\geq 1}Q_{i}}h\circ(F_{e}^{N_{e}})^{n}d\mu_{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}-\mu_{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}\Big(\bigcup_{i\geq 1}Q_{i}\Big)\int hd\mu_{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}\Big|\leq C\beta^{n-2k}\mu_{\Delta_{e}^{\prime}}\Big(\bigcup_{i\geq 1}Q_{i}\Big)||h||_{\infty}.

(5.6)

To make the exposition clearer all towers discussed in this subsection, and relations between them, are summarized in the diagrams below.
