\lxSVG@closescope {\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}\hss}\lxSVG@discardpath\lxSVG@closescope \hss}}\lxSVG@closescope\endpgfpicture}}.

Let an integer
k>m+1
, which will be determined later. For any
i=0,1,\cdots N_{e,m}-1
,

\displaystyle B_{i}=U^{-k}\pi_{e,m}^{-1}T^{-i}H,\quad\boldsymbol{B}=(\mathbbm{1}_{B_{0}},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}_{B_{N_{e,m}-1}}).

We lift now the dynamical system
(\Delta_{m},(F^{R_{m}})^{N_{e,m}},\mu_{\Delta_{m}})
to the mixing hyperbolic Young tower
(\Delta^{\prime}_{e,m},F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}},\mu_{\Delta^{\prime}_{e,m}})
, as is shown in the next lemma.

Lemma 26

\displaystyle n\cdot\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n-l})\Big]-\mathbb{E}\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}\mathbb{E}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n-l})\Big|

\displaystyle=n\cdot\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\int\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{a}}h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad-\int\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{a}}d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\int h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|.

Proof.

Note that
\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=(\mathbbm{1}_{H},\mathbbm{1}_{H}\circ T\cdots,\mathbbm{1}_{H}\circ T^{N_{e,m}-1})
,
\boldsymbol{X}_{i}=\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ T^{N_{e,m}i}
,
\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ U^{k}
. We have

\displaystyle n\cdot\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n-l})\Big]-\mathbb{E}\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}\mathbb{E}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n-l})\Big|

\displaystyle=n\cdot\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ T^{N_{e,m}p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ T^{N_{e,m}(n-l)})\Big]

\displaystyle\quad-\mathbb{E}\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}\mathbb{E}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ T^{N_{e,m}p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ T^{N_{e,m}(n-l)})\Big|

\displaystyle=n\cdot\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\int\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ\pi_{e,m}=\boldsymbol{a}}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad-\int\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ\pi_{e,m}=\boldsymbol{a}}d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\int h(\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{0}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle=n\cdot\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\int\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{a}}h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad-\int\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{a}}d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\int h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|,

where the last equality holds because
U^{k}_{*}\mu_{\Delta^{\prime}_{e,m}}=\mu_{\Delta^{\prime}_{e,m}}
.
∎

Now we will cover
\{\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{a}\}
and
B_{i}
by elements
Q\in\mathcal{Q}_{2k}^{m}
(here
k>m+1
will be determined later). Define

\displaystyle\overline{B_{i}}:=\bigcup_{Q\bigcap B_{i}\neq\emptyset}Q,\quad\partial B_{i}:=\bigcup_{Q\bigcap\overline{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}\neq\emptyset}Q,

\displaystyle A:=\{\boldsymbol{B}=\boldsymbol{a}\},\quad\overline{A}:=\bigcup_{Q\bigcap A\neq\emptyset}Q,\quad\partial A:=\bigcup_{Q\bigcap\overline{A}\setminus A\neq\emptyset}Q,

\displaystyle\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}:=(\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{B_{0}}},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{B_{N_{e,m}-1}}}),\quad\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}:=(\mathbbm{1}_{\partial{B_{0}}},\cdots,\mathbbm{1}_{\partial{B_{N_{e,m}-1}}}).

Clearly, the following properties hold

\displaystyle\overline{B_{i}}\setminus B_{i}\subseteq\partial B_{i},\quad\overline{A}\setminus A\subseteq\partial A,\quad A\subseteq\overline{A}\subseteq\{\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1\}.

Furthermore, for any
Q\subseteq\partial A
, there are
x,y\in Q
and
i\in[0,N_{e,m}-1]
such that
x\in B_{i}
, but
y\notin B_{i}
or
x\notin B_{i}
but
y\in B_{i}
. It means that
Q\subseteq\partial B_{i}
. Therefore

\overline{A}\setminus A\subseteq\partial A\subseteq\bigcup_{i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\partial B_{i}=\{\boldsymbol{\partial B}\geq 1\}.

Lemma 27

The term in Lemma
26
can be further estimated as

\displaystyle\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\int\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{A}h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}-\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(A)\int h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}[1+n\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)]\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)+\beta_{m}^{p-2k}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1),

where a constant in
``\precsim_{m}"
does not depend on
A,\boldsymbol{B}
, and
\beta_{m}
is the one in Lemma
12
.

Proof.

Using
\overline{A}\setminus A\subseteq\partial A\subseteq\{\boldsymbol{\partial B}\geq 1\}
and
A\subseteq\{\boldsymbol{B}\geq 1\}
, we have

\displaystyle\Big|\int\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{A}h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}-\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(A)\int h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}\Big[h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}-\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(A)\int h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(A)\Big|\int h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}\Big[h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{A}}h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}-\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\overline{A})\int h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\int(\mathbbm{1}_{A}-\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{A}})h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\overline{A})-\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(A)\Big|\int h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(A)\Big|\int h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}\Big[h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{A}}h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}-\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\overline{A})\int h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+2\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\partial A)+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(A)\Big|\int h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\leq\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}\Big[h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})\Big]d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|+2\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial B}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\quad+\Big|\int\mathbbm{1}_{\overline{A}}h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}-\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\overline{A})\int h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{B}\geq 1)\Big|\int h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\Big|.

Claim
:
|h(\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{n-l})-h(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{n-l})|\leq 2\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1}
.

For any
x\notin\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\{\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1\}
we have that
\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}(x)=0
for any
i\in[p,n-l]
. Then
\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\circ U^{i}(x)=\boldsymbol{B}\circ U^{i}(x)
for all
i\in[p,n-l]
. Thus, the claim holds.

Now, using Lemma
12
, and
A\subseteq\overline{A}\subseteq\{\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1\}
, we can continue the estimates as

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\int\mathbbm{1}_{A}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial B}\circ U^{i}\geq 1}d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)+\beta_{m}^{p-2k}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\overline{A})

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{B}\geq 1)\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\int\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial B}\circ U^{i}\geq 1}d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)+\beta_{m}^{p-2k}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{B}\geq 1)\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\int\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1}\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial B}\circ U^{i}\geq 1}d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}-\int\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1}d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}\int\mathbbm{1}_{\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1}d\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1)+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\quad+\beta_{m}^{p-2k}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{B}\geq 1)\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\beta_{m}^{p-2k}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\quad+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)+\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{B}\geq 1)\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\bigcup_{p\leq i\leq n-l}\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\circ U^{i}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}[1+n\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)]\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)+\beta_{m}^{p-2k}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1),

where the last “
\precsim_{m}
” is due to
\{\boldsymbol{B}\geq 1\}\subseteq\{\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1\}
. This concludes a proof of this lemma.
∎

In order to proceed with further estimates some preparations will be made. We need now to consider only
Q\in\mathcal{Q}^{m}_{2k}
, which is contained in
\partial B_{i}
for any
0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1
. By Lemma
11
, and because
i+k\mathbb{N}_{e,m}<2kN_{e,m}
, we have that
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q=\pi_{e,m}F_{e,m}^{i}U^{k}Q=\pi_{e,m}F_{e,m}^{i+kN_{e,m}}Q
does not contain singularities of
\mathbb{S}
. Therefore,
\pi_{X}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q
belongs to some
X_{t}
with
R|_{X_{t}}=R_{t}^{\prime}
, according to the Definition
2
and (
7.1
). It means that
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q\subseteq X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
.

By the definition of
\partial B_{i}
and
B_{i}
, we know that
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q\bigcap\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}\neq\emptyset
. Then there is the smallest constant
u_{t,Q}\in[0,R_{t}^{\prime})
only depending on
t,Q,m
(we drop the symbol
m
because we fix this large
m
here, see Remark
9
), such that

F^{u_{t,Q}}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q\bigcap\pi^{-1}S_{r}\neq\emptyset.

Therefore,
F^{u_{t,Q}}
pushes
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q
upward until it hits
(\pi^{-1}S_{r})\bigcap(X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0})
.

For any fixed
0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1
, we collect all
Q_{t,j}\subseteq\partial B_{i}
such that

Q_{t,j}\in\mathcal{Q}_{2k}^{m},\quad T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}(\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})\subseteq X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}.

Define
u_{t}:T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}(\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})\to\mathbb{N}_{0}
by

u_{t}(x):=\min\big\{u_{t,Q_{t,j}}:x\in T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}\text{ for some }Q_{t,j}\subseteq\partial B_{i}\big\}.

Here
u_{t}
depends on
i,k
. Since we temporarily fix
i,k
, we can drop the symbols
i,k
to simplify notations. Clearly,
F^{u_{t}}:T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}(\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})\to\Delta\bigcap(X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0})
is injective.

Lemma 28

For any
x\in T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}(\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})
, according to the definition of
u_{t}(x)
, there is a
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}
containing
x
, such that
F^{u_{t}(x)}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}\bigcap\pi^{-1}S_{r}\neq\emptyset
. Then, there exists a constant
C_{\alpha,m}>0
, which does not depend on
x,Q_{t,j},i,t,k
, such that

\diam(\pi\circ F^{u_{t}(x)}\circ T^{i}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ U^{k}Q_{t,j})\leq C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}.

Proof.

For any
\hat{\gamma}^{s}\subseteq Q_{t,j}
(here
\hat{\gamma}^{s}=\gamma^{s}\times\{l^{\prime}\}\times\{l\}
for some
\gamma^{s}\subseteq\Lambda
and
l^{\prime},l\geq 0
), suppose that
\pi_{\mathbb{N}}\big(\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{i+N_{e,m}k}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})\big)=w\leq m
,
w^{\prime}\in[0,m]
is the first non-negative number such that
\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{w^{\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})\subseteq X
, and the disks
\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{w^{\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{s}),\cdots,\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{i+N_{e,m}k}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})
visit
X
exactly
q^{\prime}
times. Then

q^{\prime}+1\geq\frac{1+i+N_{e,m}k}{m+1}\geq N_{e,m}k/(2m),\quad g^{q^{\prime}-1}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{w^{\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})=\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{i+N_{e,m}k-w}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})\subseteq X.

Therefore,
\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{i+N_{e,m}k-w}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})
is a smooth disk in
X
, and
\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{j^{\prime\prime\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})\subseteq X^{c}
for any
N_{e,m}k+i-w<j^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq N_{e,m}k+i
, and
F^{j^{\prime\prime\prime}}\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{i+N_{e,m}k}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})\subseteq X^{c}
for any
0<j^{\prime\prime\prime}\leq u_{t}(x)
. Then, by Assumption
1
and by Definition
4
, there is
C_{\alpha}^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\diam\big(\pi\circ F^{u_{t}(x)}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{i+N_{e,m}k}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\diam\big(\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{i+N_{e,m}k-w}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})\big)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C^{1+\alpha}\beta^{\alpha(q^{\prime}-1)}\diam\big(\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{w^{\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{s})\big)^{\alpha}\leq C_{\alpha}^{\prime}\beta^{\frac{\alpha N_{e,m}k}{2m}}.

On the other hand, for any
\hat{\gamma}^{u}\subseteq Q_{t,j}
(here
\hat{\gamma}^{u}=\gamma^{u}\times\{l^{\prime}\}\times\{l\}
for some
\gamma^{u}\subseteq\Lambda
and same
l^{\prime},l\geq 0
), since
N_{e,m}2k-(i+N_{e,m}k)=N_{e,m}k-i>N_{e,m}(k-1)>N_{e,m}m
, then it follows that the disks
\pi\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i}(\hat{\gamma}^{u}),\pi\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i+1}(\hat{\gamma}^{u}),\cdots,\pi\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}2k}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})
visit the base
X
at least
N_{e,m}
times. Therefore,
\pi\circ F^{u_{t}(x)}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{i+N_{e,m}k}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})
is not fully extended to
\pi\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}2k}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})
.

Assume that
\pi_{\mathbb{N}}\big(\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\big)=j^{\prime}\leq m
,
\pi_{\mathbb{N}}\big(\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}2k}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\big)=j^{\prime\prime}\leq m
, and the disks
\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i-j^{\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{u}),\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i-j^{\prime}+1}(\hat{\gamma}^{u}),\cdots,\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}2k}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})
visit
X
exactly
q
times. Then
q\in[\frac{N_{e,m}k+j^{\prime}-i+1}{m+1},N_{e,m}k+j^{\prime}-i+1]
, and

\displaystyle\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i-j^{\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\subseteq X,\quad\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i-j^{\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})=g^{-(q-1)}\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}2k-j^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\subseteq X.

By Assumption
1
, and by Definition
4
there is
C_{\alpha,m}^{\prime}>0
such that

\displaystyle\diam\big(\pi\circ F^{u_{t}(x)}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\big)

\displaystyle\leq C\diam\big(\pi\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i-j^{\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\big)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C^{1+\alpha}\beta^{\alpha(q-1)}\diam\big(\pi\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{2N_{e,m}k-j^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\big)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C^{1+\alpha}\beta^{\alpha\frac{N_{e,m}k+j^{\prime}-i}{m+1}-\alpha}\diam\big(\pi\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{2N_{e,m}k-j^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\big)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C^{1+\alpha}\beta^{\frac{\alpha N_{e,m}(k-1)}{m+1}-\alpha}\diam\big(\pi\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{2N_{e,m}k-j^{\prime\prime}}(\hat{\gamma}^{u})\big)^{\alpha}

\displaystyle\leq C_{\alpha,m}^{\prime}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}.

Similar to the argument in the proof of Lemma
12
, we have that there is a constant
C_{\alpha,m}>0
such that

\displaystyle\diam\left(\pi\circ F^{u_{t}(x)}\circ T^{i}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ U^{k}Q_{t,j}\right)=\diam\big(\pi\circ F^{u_{t}(x)}\circ\pi_{e,m}\circ F_{e,m}^{N_{e,m}k+i}Q_{t,j}\big)\leq C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}},

which concludes a proof of this lemma.
∎

Lemma 29

For any
0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1
we have
\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\partial{B_{i}})\leq C^{\prime}_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}
, where a constant
C^{\prime}_{\alpha,m}
does not depend on
i,k
.

Proof.

Consider any
x\in T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}(\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})\in\mathcal{Q}^{m}_{2k}
, where
Q_{t,j}\subseteq\partial B_{i}
. According to the definition of
u_{t}(x)
, there is a
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}
containing
x
, such that
F^{u_{t}(x)}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}\bigcap\pi^{-1}S_{r}\neq\emptyset
.

Claim
:
F^{u_{t}(x)}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}\bigcap\pi^{-1}S_{r}\neq\emptyset\implies

\displaystyle\pi F^{u_{t}(x)}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}\subseteq

\displaystyle\big[B_{r+C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}}(q)\big\backslash B_{r-C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}}(q)\big]\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]

\displaystyle\quad\bigcup N_{C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}}\Big[\partial\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}\big)\Big],

where
N_{C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}}\Big[\partial\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}\big)\Big]
is a
C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}-
neighborhood of the boundary of
\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\setminus S_{r}
, and
C_{\alpha,m}
is the one in Lemma
28
.

By the definition of
\partial B_{i}
there is
y\in Q_{t,j}
such that
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}y\notin H
. Then
F^{u_{t}(x)}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}y\notin\pi^{-1}S_{r}
(if it is not the case, then
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}y=\pi_{\Delta_{m}}F^{u_{t}(x)}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}y\in\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r}=H
). Together with
F^{u_{t}(x)}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}\bigcap\pi^{-1}S_{r}\neq\emptyset
, by Lemma
28
and Remark
5
, we can conclude that
\pi F^{u_{t}(x)}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}Q_{t,j}
is contained in a
C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}
-neighborhood of the boundaries of
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
and
\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\setminus S_{r}
. Thus, this claim holds.

If we denote
\big[B_{r+C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}}(q)\big\backslash B_{r-C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}}(q)\big]\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\bigcup N_{C_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}}\Big[\partial\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\setminus S_{r}\big)\Big]
by
\mathcal{N}_{r,q}
, then from this claim we have

Claim
:
\pi F^{u_{t}}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{r,q}
and
\mu_{\Delta}(T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})\leq\mu_{\Delta}(F^{u_{t}}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})
.

For any
x\in T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j}
,
\pi F^{u_{t}(x)}x\in\mathcal{N}_{r,q}
, so
\pi F^{u_{t}}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j}\subseteq\mathcal{N}_{r,q}
. The second relation is due to the injectivity of
F^{u_{t}}
and the measure of
\Delta
. So, the claim holds.

On the other hand, from
\frac{d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}}{d\Leb_{\mathcal{M}}}\in L^{\infty}
we have
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathcal{N}_{r,q})\leq C^{\prime}_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}
for some
C_{\alpha,m}^{\prime}>0
. By using the claims above, and the relations
U_{*}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}=\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}},T_{*}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}=\mu_{\Delta_{m}}
, we get that

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\partial{B_{i}})

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(U^{-k}\pi_{e,m}^{-1}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup Q)

\displaystyle=\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup Q)

\displaystyle\leq\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(T^{-i}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup Q)

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\mu_{\Delta}(T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{t}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})

Further, by making use of
\Delta=\Delta\bigcap\bigcup_{t}(X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0})
,
T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j}\subseteq X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0}
, and the claims above, we can continue the estimates above as

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\sum_{t}\mu_{\Delta\bigcap(X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0})}(T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\sum_{t}\mu_{\Delta\bigcap(X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0})}(F^{u_{t}}T^{i}\pi_{e,m}U^{k}\bigcup_{j}Q_{t,j})

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\sum_{t}\mu_{\Delta\bigcap(X_{t}\times\mathbb{N}_{0})}\big\{\pi^{-1}\big(\mathcal{N}_{r,q}\big)\big\}

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}\mu_{\Delta}\big\{\pi^{-1}\big(\mathcal{N}_{r,q}\big)\big\}\precsim_{m}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(\mathcal{N}_{r,q})\leq C^{\prime}_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}},

where the last line is due to
\pi_{*}\mu_{\Delta}=\mu_{\mathcal{M}}
. Hence, a proof of this lemma is concluded.
∎

Finally, we can prove now the main result of this section.

Lemma 30

If
r\to 0
, then

\displaystyle n\cdot\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n-l})\Big]-\mathbb{E}\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}\mathbb{E}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n-l})\Big|\to 0.

Proof.

At first, we estimate
\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)
and
\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)
. By making use of Lemma
29
,
U_{*}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}=\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}
and of the section
S_{r}\subseteq B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, we obtain

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\overline{B_{i}})

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(B_{i})+\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\partial{B_{i}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(U^{-k}\pi_{e,m}^{-1}T^{-i}H)+\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\partial{B_{i}})

\displaystyle=\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(H)+\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\partial{B_{i}})

\displaystyle=N_{e,m}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(H)+\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\partial{B_{i}})

\displaystyle\precsim_{\alpha,m}\mu_{\Delta}(\pi^{-1}S_{r})+\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}\precsim_{\alpha,m}r+\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}},

\displaystyle\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{0\leq i\leq N_{e,m}-1}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\partial{B_{i}})\precsim_{\alpha,m}\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}k\alpha}{2m}}.

Recall that, in (
7.6
),
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])=\int_{B_{r}(q)}\mu_{q^{\prime}}([-\pi/2,\pi/2])d\Leb_{\partial Q}
. Since
\frac{d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}}{d\Leb_{\mathcal{M}}}>0
almost surely, then
\mu_{q^{\prime}}([-\pi/2,\pi/2])>0

\Leb_{\partial Q}
-a.s.
q^{\prime}\in\partial Q
. Therefore, by the Lebesgue differentiation theorem,

\displaystyle\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])}{\Leb_{\partial Q}B_{r}(q)}=\lim_{r\to 0}\frac{\int_{B_{r}(q)}\mu_{q^{\prime}}([-\pi/2,\pi/2])d\Leb_{\partial Q}}{{\Leb_{\partial Q}B_{r}(q)}}=\mu_{q}([-\pi/2,\pi/2])>0

holds for
\Leb_{\partial Q}
-a.e.
q\in\partial Q
. Hence,

\displaystyle n^{-1}\approx\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})\approx_{m}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r})\approx_{m}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\approx_{m,q}r,

\displaystyle p=n^{1-\epsilon}\approx_{\epsilon}\mu_{\Delta_{m}}(\pi_{\Delta_{m}}\pi^{-1}S_{r})^{-1+\epsilon}\approx_{m,\epsilon}\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(S_{r})^{-1+\epsilon}\approx_{m,\epsilon}r^{-(1-\epsilon)}.

By using the estimates above, Lemmas
26
and
27
, and choosing
k=p/4
, we obtain that

\displaystyle n\cdot\sup_{h\in[0,1]}\Big|\mathbb{E}\Big[\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n-l})\Big]-\mathbb{E}\mathbbm{1}_{\boldsymbol{X}_{0}=\boldsymbol{a}}\mathbb{E}h(\boldsymbol{X}_{p},\cdots,\boldsymbol{X}_{n-l})\Big|

\displaystyle\precsim_{m}n\cdot[1+n\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)]\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\partial{B}}\geq 1)+n\beta_{m}^{p-2k}\mu_{\Delta_{e,m}^{\prime}}(\boldsymbol{\overline{B}}\geq 1)

\displaystyle\precsim_{m,\epsilon,q}r^{-1}[1+r^{-1}(r+\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}\alpha}{2m}\frac{r^{-(1-\epsilon)}}{4}})]\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}\alpha}{2m}\frac{r^{-(1-\epsilon)}}{4}}+r^{-1}(r+\beta^{\frac{N_{e,m}\alpha}{2m}\frac{r^{-(1-\epsilon)}}{4}})\beta_{m}^{\frac{r^{-(1-\epsilon)}}{2}}

converges to zero, when
r\to 0
. It concludes a proof.
∎

Proof of Theorem
2.1
.

We finished the estimates of (
6.3
) and (
6.2
) in the Lemma
15
. Thus, in view of the Remark
9
, Theorem
2.1
holds.
∎

9
Applications

9.1
A practical scheme for applications of the obtained results to concrete systems

Here we present some criteria to verify the cone conditions in Assumption
1
for two-dimensional billiards. The following notations will be used throughout this section:
x=(q,\phi)\in\mathcal{M}
,
\pi_{\partial_{\partial Q}Q}x=q
,
dx=(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}
,
\mathcal{K}=\mathcal{K}(q)
is the curvature of the boundary at a point
q\in\partial Q
. Let
f:\mathcal{M}\to\mathcal{M}
be a billiard map which maps points of the phase space at reflection times to their images at the next reflection time. It preserves an invariant measure
d\mu_{\mathcal{M}}:=(2\Leb_{\partial Q}\partial Q)^{-1}\cos\phi d\phi dq
. Let
x_{n}=(q_{n},\phi_{n})=f^{n}(x_{0})
for all
n\in\mathbb{Z}
, where
x_{0}\in\mathcal{M}
. A wave front is a smooth curve in
Q
equipped with a continuous family of unit normal vectors. Denote by
B^{+}(x_{n})
(resp.
B^{-}(x_{n})
) a curvature of a wave front right after (resp. before) the collision with the boundary
x_{n}\in\mathcal{M}
. We list now several basic formulas (see e.g.
Chernov and Markarian 2006
) for two-dimensional billiards

\displaystyle v:=d\phi/dq=B^{-}\cos\phi+\mathcal{K}=B^{+}\cos\phi-\mathcal{K},

\displaystyle 1/B^{-}(x_{n+1})=\tau_{n}+1/B^{+}(x_{n}),

\displaystyle Df(x_{n})=\frac{-1}{\cos\phi_{n+1}}\begin{bmatrix}\tau_{n}\mathcal{K}(q_{n})+\cos\phi_{n}&\tau_{n}\\
\tau_{n}\mathcal{K}(q_{n})\mathcal{K}(q_{n+1})+\mathcal{K}(q_{n})\cos\phi_{n+1}+\mathcal{K}(q_{n+1})\cos\phi_{n}\quad&\tau_{n}\mathcal{K}(q_{n+1})+\cos\phi_{n+1}\end{bmatrix},

(9.1)

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{n+1}||_{p}}{||dx_{n}||_{p}}=\frac{||Df(dx_{n})||_{p}}{||dx_{n}||_{p}}=|1+\tau_{n}B^{+}(x_{n})|,

(9.2)

\displaystyle||dx_{n}||=\frac{||dx_{n}||_{p}}{\cos\phi_{n}}\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{n}}{dq_{n}})^{2}},

(9.3)

where
\tau_{n}
is the length of the free path from
x_{n}
to
x_{n+1}
,
||dx_{n}||_{p}:=\cos\phi_{n}|dq_{n}|
,
||dx_{n}||:=\sqrt{(d\phi_{n})^{2}+(dq_{n})^{2}}
.

In what follows we always assume that all boundary components of a billiard table are at least
C^{3}
. We call boundary components with zero curvature flat components, dispersing components are convex inwards, and focusing components are convex outwards.

Lemma 31

Suppose that regular components of the boundary
\partial Q
are either flat, or dispersing, or focusing, and

each focusing component
\Gamma_{i}
is an arc of a circle, but not a full circle, and this circle does not intersect any other components of the boundary
\partial Q
. In what follows we will call this condition the simplest focusing chaos (SFC) condition.

Then there are cone fields
C^{u},C^{s}\subseteq\mathcal{T}(\mathcal{M})
, which satisfy the conditions in Assumption
1
.

Proof.

We construct
C^{u},C^{s}
fiber-wisely as follows. For any
x=(q,\phi)

\displaystyle C^{u}_{x}:=\{(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{K}\leq d\phi/dq\leq\infty\},

\displaystyle\interior{C^{u}_{x}}:=\{(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\setminus\{0\}:\mathcal{K}<d\phi/dq<\infty\}

for dispersing and flat boundary components, and

\displaystyle C^{u}_{x}:=\{(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}:\mathcal{K}\leq d\phi/dq\leq 0\},

\displaystyle\interior{C^{u}_{x}}:=\{(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\setminus\{0\}:\mathcal{K}<d\phi/dq<0\}

for focusing arcs.

\displaystyle C^{s}_{x}:=\{(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}:-\infty\leq d\phi/dq\leq-\mathcal{K}\},

\displaystyle\interior{C^{s}_{x}}:=\{(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\setminus\{0\}:-\infty<d\phi/dq<-\mathcal{K}\}

for dispersing and flat boundary components, and

\displaystyle C^{s}_{x}:=\{(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}:0\leq d\phi/dq\leq-\mathcal{K}\}

\displaystyle\interior{C^{s}_{x}}:=\{(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}\setminus\{0\}:0<d\phi/dq<-\mathcal{K}\}

for focusing arcs.

Define
C^{u}:=\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}C^{u}_{x}
,
\interior{C^{u}}:=\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}\interior{C^{u}_{x}}
,
C^{s}:=\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}C^{s}_{x}
and
\interior{C^{s}}:=\bigcup_{x\in\mathcal{M}}\interior{C^{s}_{x}}
in
\mathcal{T}\mathcal{M}
. It was shown in Theorem 8.9 of
Chernov and Markarian 2006
that
Df(C^{u}_{x})\subseteq C^{u}_{f(x)}
,
Df^{-1}(C^{s}_{x})\subseteq C^{s}_{f^{-1}(x)}
. Clearly
\interior{C^{u}}\bigcap\interior{C^{s}}=\emptyset
,
\dim(\interior{C^{u}}\bigcap\interior{C^{s}})=0<1
.

Now let
x_{0}=(q_{0},\phi_{0})\in\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
. Clearly,
\dim\big(\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\bigcap\interior{C^{u}}\big)=0<1
and
\dim\big(\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\bigcap\interior{C^{s}}\big)=0<1
. Now we will prove that the following claims hold.

Claim:

(Df)\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq C^{u}
,
(Df)\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq\interior{C^{u}}
if
\phi_{1}\neq\pm\pi/2
.

Suppose that
dx_{0}\in\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])
. Then
B^{+}(x_{0})\cos\phi_{0}-\mathcal{K}(q_{0})=d\phi_{0}/dq_{0}=\infty
, i.e.,
B^{+}(x_{0})=\infty
. It implies that

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}=\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}+1/B^{+}(x_{0})}=\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}}\in[\mathcal{K}(q_{1}),\infty).

If
q_{1}
belongs to a focusing arc, then by the SFC-condition
\tau_{0}\geq-2\mathcal{K}(q_{1})^{-1}\cos\phi_{1}
, which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}=\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}}\leq\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{-2\mathcal{K}(q_{1})^{-1}\cos\phi_{1}}=\mathcal{K}(q_{1})/2<0.

Then
dx_{1}\in C^{u}_{x_{1}}
for any
q\in\partial Q
. Particularly, if

\phi_{1}\neq\pm\pi/2
, then
\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}>\mathcal{K}(q_{1})
. Thus
dx_{1}\in\interior{C^{u}_{x_{1}}}
, and the claim holds.

Claim:

(Df)^{-1}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq C^{s}
,
(Df)^{-1}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq\interior{C^{s}}
if
\phi_{-1}\neq\pm\pi/2
.

Suppose that
dx_{0}\in\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])
. Then
B^{-}(x_{0})\cos\phi_{0}+\mathcal{K}(q_{0})=d\phi_{0}/dq_{0}=\infty
, i.e.,
B^{-}(x_{0})=\infty
. Therefore

\displaystyle 0=1/B^{-}(x_{0})=\tau_{-1}+1/B^{+}(x_{-1}),

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{-1}}{dq_{-1}}=-\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})+B^{+}(x_{-1})\cos\phi_{-1}=-\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})-\frac{\cos\phi_{-1}}{\tau_{-1}}\in(-\infty,-\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})].

If
q_{-1}
belongs to a focusing arc, then by the SFC-condition we have
\tau_{-1}\geq-2\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})^{-1}\cos\phi_{-1}
, which implies that

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{-1}}{dq_{-1}}=-\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})-\frac{\cos\phi_{-1}}{\tau_{-1}}\geq-\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})-\frac{\cos\phi_{-1}}{-2\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})^{-1}\cos\phi_{-1}}=-\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})/2>0.

Hence,
dx_{-1}\in C^{s}_{x_{-1}}
for any
q\in\partial Q
. Particularly, if

\phi_{-1}\neq\pm\pi/2
, then
\frac{d\phi_{-1}}{dq_{-1}}<-\mathcal{K}(q_{-1})
. Thus
dx_{-1}\in\interior{C^{s}_{x_{-1}}}
, which proves the claim.

Claim:
For the set
\Phi:=(\bigcup_{i\in\mathbb{Z}}f^{-i}\{\phi=\pm\pi/2\})^{c}\subseteq\mathcal{M}
we have
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(\Phi)=1
.

This claim follows from the facts that
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}
is
f
-invariant, and
\mu\{\phi=\pm\pi/2\}=0
.

Claim:
For any
x_{0}\in\Phi
we have
(Df)\interior{C^{u}_{x_{0}}}\subseteq\interior{C^{u}_{f(x_{0})}}
and
(Df)^{-1}\interior{C^{s}_{x_{0}}}\subseteq\interior{C^{s}_{f^{-1}(x_{0})}}
.

In view of the involution property of a billiard map, we just need to show that
(Df)\interior{C^{u}_{x_{0}}}\subseteq\interior{C^{u}_{f(x_{0})}}
. Let
dx_{0}\in\interior{C^{u}_{x_{0}}}
. Then

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{0}}{dq_{0}}=-\mathcal{K}(q_{0})+B^{+}(x_{0})\cos\phi_{0}\in(\mathcal{K}(q_{0}),\infty)\text{ if }\mathcal{K}(q_{0})\geq 0,

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{0}}{dq_{0}}=-\mathcal{K}(q_{0})+B^{+}(x_{0})\cos\phi_{0}\in(\mathcal{K}(q_{0}),0)\text{ if }\mathcal{K}(q_{0})<0.

In order to prove the relation
dx_{1}\in\interior{C^{u}_{x_{1}}}
, we will show that

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}=\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}+1/B^{+}(x_{0})}\in(\mathcal{K}(q_{1}),\infty)\text{ if }\mathcal{K}(q_{1})\geq 0,

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}=\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}+1/B^{+}(x_{0})}\in(\mathcal{K}(q_{1}),0)\text{ if }\mathcal{K}(q_{1})<0,

for case by case, depending on the positions of
q_{0},q_{1}
, where
x_{0}\in\Phi
.

If
\mathcal{K}(q_{0})\geq 0
and
\mathcal{K}(q_{1})\geq 0
, then
B^{+}(x_{0})>0
,
d\phi_{1}/dq_{1}\in(0,\infty)
.

If now
\mathcal{K}(q_{0})\geq 0
and
\mathcal{K}(q_{1})<0
, then
B^{+}(x_{0})>0
,
d\phi_{1}/dq_{1}>\mathcal{K}(q_{1})
, and by the SFC-condition we get

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}<\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}}\leq\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{-2\mathcal{K}(q_{1})^{-1}\cos\phi_{1}}\leq\mathcal{K}(q_{1})/2<0.

If
\mathcal{K}(q_{0})<0
and
\mathcal{K}(q_{1})\geq 0
, then
B^{+}(x_{0})\in\big(\frac{2\mathcal{K}(q_{0})}{\cos\phi_{0}},\frac{\mathcal{K}(q_{0})}{\cos\phi_{0}}\big)\subseteq(-\infty,0)
, and by the SFC-condition

\displaystyle\tau_{0}+1/B^{+}(x_{0})>\tau_{0}+\mathcal{K}(q_{0})^{-1}\cos\phi_{0}\geq-2\mathcal{K}(q_{0})^{-1}\cos\phi_{0}+\mathcal{K}(q_{0})^{-1}\cos\phi_{0}>0

\displaystyle\implies\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}=\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}+1/B^{+}(x_{0})}\in(\mathcal{K}(q_{1}),\infty).

If
\mathcal{K}(q_{0})<0
and
\mathcal{K}(q_{1})<0
, then
B^{+}(x_{0})\in\big(\frac{2\mathcal{K}(q_{0})}{\cos\phi_{0}},\frac{\mathcal{K}(q_{0})}{\cos\phi_{0}}\big)
,
\tau_{0}+1/B^{+}(x_{0})>0
and

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}=\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}+1/B^{+}(x_{0})}>\mathcal{K}(q_{1}).

According to the SFC-condition (i.e.,
\tau_{0}\geq-\mathcal{K}(q_{0})^{-1}\cos\phi_{0}-\mathcal{K}(q_{1})^{-1}\cos\phi_{1}
),

\displaystyle\frac{d\phi_{1}}{dq_{1}}<\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{\tau_{0}+\mathcal{K}(q_{0})^{-1}\cos\phi_{0}}\leq\mathcal{K}(q_{1})+\frac{\cos\phi_{1}}{-\mathcal{K}(q_{1})^{-1}\cos\phi_{1}}=0.

Therefore
dx_{1}\in\interior{C^{u}_{x_{1}}}
, and the claim holds.

Combining all the claims above, we obtain that for any
x_{0}=(q,\phi_{0})\in\Phi
,
n\geq 1
,

\displaystyle(Df)^{n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq\interior{C^{u}},\quad(Df)^{-n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq\interior{C^{s}}.

Claim:
For a.e.
q\in\partial Q
, we have

\displaystyle(Df)^{n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq\interior{C^{u}},\quad(Df)^{-n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\subseteq\interior{C^{s}}.

If it is not the case, then there exists a subset
O\subseteq\partial Q
with
\Leb_{\partial Q}O>0
, so that for any
q\in O
,

\displaystyle(Df)^{n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\nsubseteq\interior{C^{u}}\text{ or }(Df)^{-n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\nsubseteq\interior{C^{s}}.

Then
\mu_{\mathcal{M}}(O\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])>0
,
(O\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\bigcap\Phi\neq\emptyset
, and for any
x_{0}=(q,\phi_{0})\in(O\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\bigcap\Phi
,

\displaystyle(Df)^{n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\nsubseteq\interior{C^{u}}\text{ or }(Df)^{-n}\mathcal{T}(\{q\}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\nsubseteq\interior{C^{s}}.

Hence, we came to a contradiction, and the claim holds, which concludes a proof of this lemma.∎

All billiard systems, which will be considered below, satisfy the conditions of Lemma
31
. Therefore the condition on existence of the cone fields in Assumption
1
holds automatically.

9.2
Sinai and Diamond billiards

Pictures of billiard tables of Sinai billiards and of diamond billiards are presented in Figures
2
and
2
. Choose the first return time
R=1
. The Assumption
1
in this case holds automatically.

Figure 1:
Sinai billiard

Figure 2:
Diamond billiard

Corollary 2

Theorem
2.1
holds for Sinai billiards with a bounded or unbounded horizon (see e.g.
Chernov and Markarian 2006
), and for diamond billiards (see e.g.
Pène and Saussol 2020
).

Remark 12
.

In fact, if a billiard map of a two-dimensional billiard has a CMZ structure, i.e.,
R=1
, and if the boundary of a billiard table satisfies the conditions of Lemma
31
, then Theorem
2.1
holds. Moreover, such billiards have exponentially mixing rates (or exponential decay of correlations), i.e., of order
O(\rho^{n})
for some
\rho\in(0,1)
.

In the following subsections, we consider two-dimensional slowly mixing billiards, which were studied in
Chernov and Zhang 2008
,
Chernov and Zhang 2005
. The rates of mixing (decay of correlations) for these billiards are either of order
O(n^{-1})
, or
O(n^{-2})
.

9.3
Squashes or Stadium-type billiards

A billiard table
Q
of a squash billiard is a convex domain bounded by two circular arcs and two straight (flat) segments tangent to the arcs at their common endpoints. A squash billiard is called a stadium if flat sides are parallel, see Figure
4
. Initially called squash billiards, they were later sometimes called “skewed” stadia, drive-belt billiards, etc, see Figure
4
. Note that squashes contain a boundary arc, which is longer than a half circle. We will verify now the Assumption
1
for this class of billiards.

Figure 3:
Stadium billiard

Figure 4:
Squash billiard

Let for a (“straight”) stadium
X\subseteq\mathcal{M}
be the region where the first collisions (in a series of consecutive collisions with one and the same circular arc) with circular arcs occur. Denote by
R
the first return time to
X
for the billiard map
f
. Let
l
be the length of each straight segment in the boundary of a billiard table. Without loss of generality, we may assume that the radius of circular arcs equals
1
.

1.

\bigcup_{i\geq 1}\partial\{x\in X:R(x)=i\}\subseteq\mathbb{S}
. Note that, if
R
varies between
i
and
i+1
, then a collision must occur at the endpoints of two circular arcs. It follows from (
9.1
) that
\mathcal{K}
has a jump after such collisions. The singularities of
f^{R}
appear only because of this type of collisions with the boundary.

2.

B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section. Consider the map
\pi_{X}:\pi^{-1}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)\to X
. Let it be non-injective. Then there are
x_{1},x_{2}\in\pi^{-1}\big(B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]\big)
, such that
\pi_{X}x_{1}=\pi_{X}x_{2}=x\in X
. This implies that there are
0\leq n_{1}<n_{2}<R(x)
, such that
f^{n_{1}}x=x_{1}
,
f^{n_{2}}x=x_{2}
, and
d(q,\pi_{\partial Q}x_{1})<r
,
d(q,\pi_{\partial Q}x_{2})<r
, where
x
is a point of the first collision (in a series) with one of the circular arcs.

Suppose that
q
belongs to a circular arc, and
r
is sufficiently small. Then
\pi_{\partial}x_{1}
and
\pi_{\partial}x_{2}
belong to a
r
-neighborhood of
q
. So the orbits of
x_{1}
and of
x_{2}
are sliding along the same circular arc, and
f^{n_{2}-n_{1}}x_{1}=x_{2}
. Hence the angle
|\phi|
for
x_{1}
(and
x_{2}
) is greater or equal to
\pi/2-2r/(2n_{2}-2n_{1})\geq\pi/2-r
. Therefore, a “non-injective” configuration is
\{(q^{\prime},\phi)\in\mathcal{M}:d(q,q^{\prime})<r,|\phi|>\pi/2-r\}
. Clearly, it has a measure of order
O(r^{3})
.

Suppose now that
q
belongs to the flat part of the boundary, and that
r
is sufficiently small. Since the collisions at
x_{1},x_{2}
occur after the first collision at
x
on a circular arc and there is no reflection off another circular arc yet, then
x_{1},x_{2}
must be bouncing on the boundary component, which contains
q
. Since the radius of the circular arc is
1
, and at these points the angle of reflection
|\phi|
is the same, then
|\phi|
does not exceed
\arctan[2r/(2n_{2}-2n_{1})]\leq\arctan(r)
. Therefore, the set of “non-injective” configurations in this case is
\{(q^{\prime},\phi)\in\mathcal{M}:d(q,q^{\prime})<r,|\phi|<\arctan(r)\}
, which has a measure of order
O(r^{2})
.

Summarizing the arguments above, we have that the set of “non-injective” configurations has a measure of order
O(r^{2})
. Therefore
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section.

3.

Hölder continuity along small (un)stable manifolds. The reason here for using “small” manifolds is that in Definition
3
(un)stable manifolds are supposed to be maximal, and may not be Hölder continuous. Therefore, besides the singularities
\mathbb{S}_{1}
of
f^{R}
, other points in
X
have to be added into
\mathbb{S}
(see Remark
1
). We define
\mathbb{S}
by
(f^{R})^{-1}\mathbb{S}_{1}\bigcup\mathbb{S}_{1}\bigcup f^{R}\mathbb{S}_{1}
, and define “small” (un)stable manifolds in the same way as that in Definition
3
.

First we consider a stable manifold with the stable cone

\displaystyle C^{s}(x):=\{dx=(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}:

\displaystyle B^{+}(x)\in[-\frac{1}{\cos\phi},0]\text{ if }\mathcal{K}(q)=-1,

\displaystyle B^{+}(x)\in[-\infty,0]\text{ if }\mathcal{K}(q)=0\}.

Suppose that the first collision on a circular arc is at a point
x_{0}\in X
, and its stable manifold is
\gamma^{s}(x_{0})
. Several cases must be considered.

(a)

Sliding along a circular arc, i.e., the points
x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}

(k\geq 0)
belong to the same circular arc, and
x_{k+1}
does not. Then
dx_{k}\in C^{s}(x_{k})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{k})=\frac{-2}{\cos\phi_{k}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{k-1}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k-1})}}\in[-\frac{1}{\cos\phi_{k}},0]

\displaystyle\implies B^{+}(x_{k-1})\in[\frac{-1}{\tau_{k-1}-\cos\phi_{k}},\frac{-1}{\tau_{k-1}-\frac{\cos\phi_{k}}{2}}]=[\frac{-1}{\cos\phi_{0}},\frac{-1}{\frac{3\cos\phi_{0}}{2}}].

Inductively, we have for any
i\in[0,k]
,

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{i})\in\big[\frac{-1}{\cos\phi_{0}},\frac{-2(k-i)}{[2(k-i)+1]\cos\phi_{0}}\big],

and for any
i\in[0,k)
,

\displaystyle|1+\tau_{i}B^{+}(x_{i})|\leq 1.

By (
9.2
) and (
9.3
), for any
i\in[0,k]
,

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}=\prod_{j\leq i-1}|1+\tau_{j}B^{+}(x_{j})|\leq 1,

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||}{||dx_{0}||}=\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}\frac{\cos\phi_{0}}{\cos\phi_{i}}\frac{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{i}}{dq_{i}})^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{0}}{dq_{0}})^{2}}}\precsim 1,

where the last
``\precsim"
holds thanks to the fact that on a circular arc
\cos\phi_{0}=\cos\phi_{i}
, and a slope of the stable manifold is uniformly bounded. Therefore,
f^{i}|_{\gamma^{s}(x_{0})}
is Lipschitz in this case.

(b)

Suppose that
x_{k+1},\cdots,x_{k+n}
,
(n>1)
are on the flat sides, and
x_{k+n+1}
is on another circular arc. Then

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{k+n+1})=\frac{-2}{\cos\phi_{k+n+1}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{k+n}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k+n})}}\in[-\frac{1}{\cos\phi_{k+n+1}},0]

\displaystyle\implies B^{+}(x_{n+k})\in[\frac{-1}{\tau_{n+k}-\cos\phi_{n+k+1}},\frac{-1}{\tau_{n+k}-\frac{\cos\phi_{n+k+1}}{2}}].

Observe now that
\mathcal{K}(q_{n+k})=0
. Then

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{k+n})=\frac{0}{\cos\phi_{k+n}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{k+n-1}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k+n-1})}}\in[\frac{-1}{\tau_{n+k}-\cos\phi_{n+k+1}},\frac{-1}{\tau_{n+k}-\frac{\cos\phi_{n+k+1}}{2}}]

\displaystyle\implies B^{+}(x_{n+k-1})\in[\frac{-1}{\tau_{n+k}+\tau_{n+k-1}-\cos\phi_{n+k+1}},\frac{-1}{\tau_{n+k}+\tau_{n+k-1}-\frac{\cos\phi_{n+k+1}}{2}}].

Inductively, we have for any
0\leq i<n

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{n+k-i})\in[\frac{-1}{\sum_{j\leq i}\tau_{n+k-j}-\cos\phi_{n+k+1}},\frac{-1}{\sum_{j\leq i}\tau_{n+k-j}-\frac{\cos\phi_{n+k+1}}{2}}],

\displaystyle|1+\tau_{n+k-i}B^{+}(x_{n+k-i})|\leq 1,

where the last inequality is due to the SFC-condition, i.e.,
\tau_{n+k}\geq 2\cos\phi_{n+k+1}
.

By (
9.2
), for any
i\in[k+1,k+n]
,

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}=\prod_{j\leq i-1}|1+\tau_{j}B^{+}(x_{j})|\leq 1.

Since
n>1
, then
|\phi_{i}|
is uniformly bounded away from
\pi/2
. Thus

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||}{||dx_{0}||}=\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}\frac{\cos\phi_{0}}{\cos\phi_{i}}\frac{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{i}}{dq_{i}})^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{0}}{dq_{0}})^{2}}}\precsim 1,

where the last
``\precsim"
holds due to the fact that a slope of a stable manifold is uniformly bounded. Therefore,
f^{i}|_{\gamma^{s}(x_{0})}
in this case is Lipschitz too.

(c)

Consider now transitions between circular arcs through a flat side, i.e.,
x_{k}
is the last in a series collision on a circular arc, and
x_{k+1}
is on a flat side, while
x_{k+2}
is on another circular arc. We have

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{k+1}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}=\prod_{j\leq k}|1+\tau_{j}B^{+}(x_{j})|\leq 1,

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{k+1}||}{||dx_{0}||}=\frac{||dx_{k+1}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}\frac{\cos\phi_{0}}{\cos\phi_{k+1}}\frac{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{k+1}}{dq_{k+1}})^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{0}}{dq_{0}})^{2}}}\precsim\frac{\cos\phi_{0}}{\cos\phi_{k+1}}=\frac{\cos\phi_{k}}{\cos\phi_{k+1}}.

For each
\phi_{k}
a possible minimum of
\cos\phi_{k+1}
(i.e., the maximum of
\phi_{k+1}
) satisfies the relation

\displaystyle\cos^{2}\phi_{k}=(1-l^{2})\cos^{2}\max\phi_{k+1}+2l\cos\max\phi_{k+1}\sin\max\phi_{k+1},

which holds if
q_{k+1}
is at the end point of this flat side (or at the end point of another circular arc). Thus

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{k+1}||}{||dx_{0}||}\precsim_{l}\frac{1}{\cos\phi_{k}}\precsim k.

Since
\diam\gamma^{s}(x_{0})=O(1/k^{2})
, we have

\displaystyle\diam f^{k+1}\big(\gamma^{s}(x_{0})\big)\precsim k\diam\gamma^{s}(x_{0})\precsim[\diam\gamma^{s}(x_{0})]^{1/2}.

The arguments above show that there exists
C>0
, such that for any
\gamma^{s}\subseteq\big(\bigcup_{i\geq-1}(f^{R})^{-i}\mathbb{S}_{1}\big)^{c}
,

\displaystyle d_{f^{j}\gamma^{s}}(f^{j}x,f^{j}y)\leq Cd_{\gamma^{s}}(x,y)^{1/2}\text{ for all }j<R(x).

Next we turn to unstable manifolds, and to the unstable cone field

\displaystyle C^{u}(x):=\{dx=(dq,d\phi)\in\mathcal{T}_{x}\mathcal{M}:

\displaystyle B^{+}(x)\in[-\frac{2}{\cos\phi},-\frac{1}{\cos\phi}]\text{ if }\mathcal{K}(q)=-1,

\displaystyle B^{+}(x)\in[0,\infty]\text{ if }\mathcal{K}(q)=0\}.

Suppose that the first collision in a series on a circular arc is at
x_{0}\in X
. The unstable manifold at this point is
\gamma^{u}(x_{0})
. Again we will consider several cases.

(a)

Sliding along a circular arc, i.e.,
x_{0},x_{1},\cdots,x_{k}

(k\geq 0)
belong to one and the same circular arc, while
x_{k+1}
does not. Then
dx_{0}\in C^{u}(x_{0})
, i.e.,

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{1})=\frac{-2}{\cos\phi_{1}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{0}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{0})}},\quad B^{+}(x_{0})\in[-\frac{2}{\cos\phi_{0}},-\frac{1}{\cos\phi_{0}}],

\displaystyle\implies B^{+}(x_{1})\in[-\frac{4}{3\cos\phi_{1}},-\frac{1}{\cos\phi_{1}}].

Inductively, we have for any
i\in[0,k]
,

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{i})\in\big[-\frac{2i+2}{(2i+1)\cos\phi_{i}},-\frac{1}{\cos\phi_{i}}\big],

and for any
i\in[0,k)
,

\displaystyle|1+\tau_{i}B^{+}(x_{i})|\leq\frac{2i+3}{2i+1}.

By (
9.2
) and (
9.3
), for any
i\in[0,k)
,

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}=\prod_{j\leq i-1}|1+\tau_{j}B^{+}(x_{j})|\precsim i\leq k,

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||}{||dx_{0}||}=\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}\frac{\cos\phi_{0}}{\cos\phi_{i}}\frac{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{i}}{dq_{i}})^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{0}}{dq_{0}})^{2}}}\precsim k,

where the last
``\precsim"
holds because
\cos\phi_{0}=\cos\phi_{i}
on a circular arc, and the slope of a stable manifold is uniformly bounded.

Our small unstable manifold
\gamma^{u}(x_{0})
is contained in a connected component of the set
\big(\bigcup_{i\geq-1}(f^{R})^{i}\mathbb{S}_{1}\big)^{c}
. Thus the length of
\gamma^{u}(x_{0})
is
O(k^{-2})
. Hence,

\displaystyle\diam f^{i}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)\precsim k\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\precsim[\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{1/2}.

Therefore,
f^{i}|_{\gamma^{s}(x_{0})}
is Hölder continuous.

(b)

Bouncing on flat sides. Here the points
x_{k+1},\cdots,x_{k+n}
,
(n>1)
are on flat sides, and
x_{k+n+1}
belongs to another circular arc.

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{k+1})=\frac{0}{\cos\phi_{k+1}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{k}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k})}},\quad B^{+}(x_{k})\in[-\frac{2k+2}{(2k+1)\cos\phi_{k}},-\frac{1}{\cos\phi_{k}}],

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{k+2})=\frac{0}{\cos\phi_{k+2}}+\frac{1}{\tau_{k+1}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k+1})}}=\frac{1}{\tau_{k+1}+\tau_{k}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k})}}.

Inductively, we have for any
0\leq i\leq n

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{k+i})=\frac{1}{\sum_{0\leq j\leq i-1}\tau_{k+j}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k})}},

\displaystyle|1+\tau_{k+i}B^{+}(x_{k+i})|=\big|\frac{\sum_{0\leq j\leq i}\tau_{k+j}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k})}}{\sum_{0\leq j\leq i-1}\tau_{k+j}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k})}}\big|.

Since
n>1
, then
k
may assume only a finite number of values. Then, it is bounded by a constant depending on
Q
only, while
|\phi_{k}|,\cdots,|\phi_{k+n}|
are bounded away from
\pi/2
by a positive constant, which also depends only on
Q
. By (
9.2
) for any
i\in[k+1,k+n]
,

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}=\prod_{j\leq i-1}|1+\tau_{j}B^{+}(x_{j})|\precsim\big|\frac{\sum_{0\leq j\leq i}\tau_{k+j}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k})}}{\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k})}}\big|\precsim n,

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||}{||dx_{0}||}=\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}\frac{\cos\phi_{0}}{\cos\phi_{i}}\frac{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{i}}{dq_{i}})^{2}}}{\sqrt{1+(\frac{d\phi_{0}}{dq_{0}})^{2}}}\precsim n,

where the last
``\precsim"
holds thanks to the fact that the slope of a stable manifold is uniformly bounded.

A small unstable manifold
\gamma^{u}(x_{0})
is contained in a connected component of
\big(\bigcup_{i\geq-1}(f^{R})^{i}\mathbb{S}_{1}\big)^{c}
. Thus the length of
\gamma^{u}(x_{0})
is
O(n^{-2})
. Hence

\displaystyle\diam f^{i}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)\precsim n\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\precsim[\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{1/2}.

Therefore
f^{i}|_{\gamma^{s}(x_{0})}
is Hölder continuous.

(c)

Sliding on a flat side, i.e,
x_{k}
corresponds to a point of the last collision with a circular arc,
x_{k+1}
is on a flat side, and
x_{k+2}
is on another circular arc. We have

\displaystyle B^{+}(x_{k})\in[-\frac{2k+2}{(2k+1)\cos\phi_{k}},-\frac{1}{\cos\phi_{k}}],

\displaystyle\implies\frac{||dx_{k+2}||_{p}}{||dx_{k+1}||_{p}}=|1+\tau_{k+1}B^{+}(x_{k+1})|=\Big|1+\frac{\tau_{k+1}}{\tau_{k}+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{k})}}\Big|\geq\frac{\tau_{k}+\tau_{k+1}-\frac{2k+1}{2k+2}\cos\phi_{k}}{\tau_{k}-\frac{2k+1}{2k+2}\cos\phi_{k}}\geq 1,

where the last
``\geq"
is due to the SFC-condition. Therefore

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{k+2}||}{||dx_{k+1}||}\approx\frac{||dx_{k+2}||_{p}}{||dx_{k+1}||_{p}}\frac{\cos\phi_{k+1}}{\cos\phi_{k+2}}\succsim\frac{\cos\phi_{k+1}}{\cos\phi_{k+2}}\succsim\cos\phi_{k+2},

where the argument for the last
``\succsim"
is the same as that for the case of “sliding on the flat sides” for stable manifolds. Since
\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\subseteq\big(\bigcup_{i\geq-1}(f^{R})^{i}\mathbb{S}_{1}\big)^{c}
, then
f^{k+2}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})
belongs to a
k^{\prime}
-sliding cell,
\diam f^{k+2}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)=O(1/k^{\prime 2})
and
\cos\phi_{k+2}\approx 1/k^{\prime}
, so
\frac{||dx_{k+2}||}{||dx_{k+1}||}\succsim\cos\phi_{k+2}\approx 1/k^{\prime}
. From Lemma 8.45 of
Chernov and Markarian 2006
we have

\displaystyle\diam f^{k+1}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)\precsim k^{\prime}\diam f^{k+2}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)\precsim[\diam f^{k+2}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)]^{1/2}\precsim[\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{1/8}.

The arguments above show that there is
C>0
such that for any
\gamma^{u}\subseteq\big(\bigcup_{i\geq-1}(f^{R})^{i}\mathbb{S}_{1}\big)^{c}

\displaystyle d_{f^{j}\gamma^{u}}(f^{j}x,f^{j}y)\leq Cd_{\gamma^{u}}(x,y)^{1/8}\text{ for all }j<R(x).

Now we turn to a squash (or a “skewed” stadium). Since two flat sides are not parallel, we can suppose that the angle between them is
\gamma>0
. Following
Chernov and Zhang 2005
, define
X
to be the same set as that for “straight” stadiums. Verification of Assumption
1
is the same as for a “straight” stadium. Therefore we will skip the details and outline only the differences.

1.

B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section. If
q
is on the flat sides, then the angle of reflection increases or decreases by
\gamma
between two consecutive bounces on flat sides. Then
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a section, provided that
r
is much smaller than
\gamma
.

Let now a point
q
belongs to a circular arc. The analysis in the case for orbits, sliding on these arcs, is the same as the one for a straight stadium. Another case is a bouncing on a circular arc. It is enough to consider this case for the bigger arc only. Let us estimate a measure of the set of “non-injective” configurations. Suppose that
(p_{1},\phi_{1})=f^{n}(x)
and
(p_{2},\phi_{2})=f^{m}(x)
, where
n<m
,
d(q,p_{1})<r
,
d(q,p_{2})<r
,
x=(p_{0},\theta)\in X
,
n,m<R(x)
, and
\theta
is sufficiently small. Assume that
r
is so small that all points
q,p_{0},p_{1},p_{2}
are on the bigger arc. From the elementary geometry we have
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=\theta
,
d(p_{0},p_{1})=4n\theta
,
d(p_{0},p_{2})=4m\theta
, and
d(p_{1},p_{2})=4(m-n)\theta
. Therefore,
d(p_{1},p_{2})=4(m-n)\theta<2r
, which implies that
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=\theta<r/2
. Thus the set of “non-injective” configurations for bouncing on the circular arc is contained in
\{(p,\phi)\in\mathcal{M}:p\in B_{r}(q),|\phi|<r/2\}
, which has a measure of order
O(r^{2})
.

2.

Another difference is in verification of Hölder continuity. Besides the three cases studied for the (straight) stadiums, we also need to consider here a bouncing on the bigger circular arc. The argument there is exactly the same as that for “sliding on a circular arc”.

We can conclude now this subsection by stating the following

Corollary 3

Theorem
2.1
holds for squash billiards (stadiums with unequal or equal focusing arcs).

9.4
Another class of billiards with focusing components

In this section we consider billiard tables
Q
for which each smooth component
\Gamma_{i}\subseteq\partial Q
of the boundary is either dispersing, i.e., convex inwards, or focusing, i.e., convex
outwards. A curvature of every dispersing component is bounded
away from zero and infinity. We assume that every focusing component is an arc of a circle, and that there are no points of
\partial Q
on that circle or inside it, other than the arc itself (that is, the SFC-condition). We assume also that two dispersing components intersect (if they do) transversally (i.e., there are no cusps) and, besides, every focusing arc is not longer than a half of the corresponding circle, e.g. see the Figure
6
.
Denote the union of dispersing components by
\partial Q^{+}
, and the union of focusing components by
\partial Q^{-}
. Let
X\subseteq\mathcal{M}
be

\displaystyle X:=(\partial Q^{+}\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2])\bigcup\{x\in\mathcal{M}:\pi_{\partial Q}x\in\partial Q^{-},\pi_{\partial Q}x\text{ and }\pi_{\partial Q}(f^{-1}x)\text{ belong to different }\Gamma_{i}\},

i.e., only the first collisions on circular arcs and any collisions with the dispersing components are included in
X
. Therefore, the case with
R>1
may occur only in the series of reflections off a circular arc.

Figure 5:
Flower billiard

Figure 6:
Semi-dispersing billiard

The verification of Assumption
1
is similar (actually it is easier) to the one for stadium billiards. Therefore we just outline below the differences.

1.

B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section. If a point
q
belongs to dispersing components, then clearly
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a section. If
q
belongs to circular arc, then
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section. The argument in this case is exactly the same as for stadium billiards.

2.

Hölder continuity. Note that
R(x)>1
only occurs if
\pi_{\partial Q}x\in\partial Q^{-}
. In this case, the orbit
\{x,f(x),f^{2}x\cdots f^{R-1}(x)\}
is a series of consecutive reflections off a circular arc. Thus the argument for Hölder continuity is exactly the same as that for stadium-type billiards.

Now we can conclude this subsection by stating the following

Corollary 4

Theorem
2.1
holds for the class of billiards analyzed in this subsection.

9.5
Semi-dispersing billiards

In this subsection we consider billiard tables of the following type. Let
R_{0}\subseteq\mathbb{R}^{2}
be a rectangle, and scatterers
B_{1},\cdots,B_{r}\subseteq\interior R_{0}
are open strictly convex sub-domains with smooth, (at least
C^{3})
, or piece-wise smooth boundaries whose curvature is bounded away from zero,
and such that
B_{i}\bigcap B_{j}=\emptyset
for
i\neq j
. The boundary of a billiard table
Q=R_{0}\setminus\bigcup_{i}B_{i}
is partially dispersing (convex inwards) and partially neutral (flat), e.g. see Figure
6
. The flat part is
\partial R_{0}
.

Denote by
\partial Q^{+}
the union of dispersing components, and the union of four flat sides by
\partial R_{0}
. Let
X:=\{x\in\mathcal{M}:\pi_{\partial Q}x\in\partial Q^{+}\}
, where
R
is the first return time to
X
. If
\sup R<\infty
, then this billiard system has exponential decay of correlations, i.e., it is not slowly mixing. So we assume that
\sup R=\infty
. Clearly
f^{R}
is a billiard map of a Sinai billiard with an infinite horizon. Verification of the Assumption
1
for such billiards has a few differences with the one for stadium billiards. Namely

1.

clearly,
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a section if
q\in\partial Q^{+}
. We will show that, if
q\in\partial R_{0}
, then
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section. Without loss of generality, we may assume that
R_{0}=[0,1]^{2}
,
q=(0,c)\in\partial R_{0}
, where
c\in(0,1)
. Unfold now the bounded billiard table
Q
to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
by mirror reflections after collisions with the flat boundary. Then a billiard orbit, which is a broken line in
Q
, is lifted to become a straight line in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
, and scatterers
B_{1},\cdots,B_{r}
are lifted to generate a periodic configuration of scatterers in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
. (Note that this trick is the same as the one used for Sinai billiards with infinite horizon. Namely, the analysis of a billiard flow between two reflections off scatterers in
R_{0}
reduces to consideration of straight segments between consecutive reflections off scatterers in
\mathbb{R}^{2}
). Suppose that a point
q
is lifted in this way to the points
\{(p,c+k):p,k\in\mathbb{Z}\}
.

In order to prove that
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section for sufficiently small
r>0
, we will study the “non-injective” part, i.e., a measure of the configuration
(q_{1},\phi_{1})\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
, which satisfies
f^{n}(q_{1},\phi_{1})=(q_{2},\phi_{2})
for some
n\geq 1,(q_{2},\phi_{2})\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
. A billiard orbit, which is moving along the following set of points

(q_{1},\phi_{1}),f(q_{1},\phi_{1}),\cdots,f^{n-1}(q_{1},\phi_{1}),f^{n}(q_{1},\phi_{1})=(q_{2},\phi_{2}),

does not intersect
\bigcup_{i}B_{i}
. The lifting to
\mathbb{R}^{2}
has the following property. There are
p,k\in\mathbb{Z}
(which depend on
\phi_{1},n
) such that the line between
q_{1}\in B_{r}(q)
and
q_{2}+(p,k)\in B_{r}(q+(p,k))
does not intersect the periodic configuration of the scatterers, and the slope of this line is

\tan\phi_{1}=k/p+O(r)=\frac{k/\gcd(k,p)}{p/\gcd(k,p)}+O(r).

Since this line intersects
B_{r}(q)
and
B_{r}(q+(p,k))
, then it intersects
B_{r}(q+(p/i,k/i))
for any
i
, such that
i|p
and
i|k
, in particular,
i=\gcd(p,k)
. Therefore every “non-injective” configuration
(q_{1},\phi_{1})\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
corresponds to unique direction vector
(p/\gcd(k,p),k/\gcd(k,p))
. Without loss of generality, we assume that
\gcd(k,p)=1
. Here
p
means that the line between
q_{1}
and
q_{2}+(p,k)
intersects the vertical boundary
\approx p
times, which implies that the billiard flow in
Q
divides
R_{0}
into several pieces with measures of order
O(1/p)
. Let
k
mean that the line between
q_{1}
and
q_{2}+(p,k)
intersects the horizontal boundary
\approx k
times, which implies that the billiard flow in
Q
divides
R_{0}
into several pieces with measure of order
O(1/k)
. If
p
or
k
is larger than
M>0
(which depends on the size of
\bigcup_{i}B_{i}
), then the billiard orbit, which is passing consecutively through the points

(q_{1},\phi_{1}),f(q_{1},\phi_{1}),\cdots,f^{n-1}(q_{1},\phi_{1}),f^{n}(q_{1},\phi_{1})=(q_{2},\phi_{2})

must intersect
\bigcup_{i}B_{i}
. Therefore
\max\{p,k\}
must not exceed
M
for such billiard orbit, in order for it not to intersect
\bigcup_{i}B_{i}
. It implies that there are finitely many pairs
(p,k)
with
\gcd(p,k)=1
such that
\tan\phi_{1}=k/p+O(r)
. Then
\phi_{1}
has a measure of order
O(r)
. So, a measure of “non-injective” configuration
(q_{1},\phi_{1})\in B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is of order
O(r^{2})
. Hence
B_{r}(q)\times[-\pi/2,\pi/2]
is a quasi-section.

2.

Hölder continuity. Consider, at first, unstable manifolds in
X
(which are, actually, homogeneous unstable manifolds, defined, e.g., in section 5.4 of
Chernov and Markarian 2006
, see also Remark
1
). Suppose that
x_{0}\in X
, and that
n:=R(x_{0})
is sufficiently large. Then the orbit of
x_{0}
hits
\partial R_{0}
many times before getting back to
\partial Q^{+}
. Clearly for billiards of this type all possible angles
\phi_{1}
are bounded by
\pi/2
. Consider now the worse case when
\phi_{1}\approx 0
, i.e.,
|\phi_{1}|<\phi_{Q}
for some small
\phi_{Q}
, (which depends only on exact shape of a billiard table under consideration), such that
\phi_{1}=\phi_{2}=\cdots=\phi_{j-1}=\phi_{j+1}=\cdots=\phi_{n-1}
, and
\phi_{j}=\pi/2-\phi_{1}\approx\pi/2
for some
2\leq j<n
. Then for any
i\neq j
we have
\cos\phi_{j}\approx 1/n
, and
\cos\phi_{i}\approx 1
. Assume now that
f^{n}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)
belongs to a homogeneity strip (see its definition on page 536 in
Chernov 1999
), for instance to
\cos\phi_{n}\approx 1/k^{2}
. Observe also that
k\succsim n^{1/4}
(see page 544 of
Chernov 1999
). It is known, see e.g.
Chernov and Markarian 2006
, that
B^{+}(x_{0})\in[\frac{2K(q_{0})}{\cos\phi_{0}},\frac{2K(q_{0})}{\cos\phi_{0}}+O(1)]
. Thus we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{j})}=\frac{1}{B^{-}(x_{i})}=\sum_{0\leq i\leq j-1}\tau(f^{i}x_{0})+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{0})}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{||dx_{n}||_{p}}{||dx_{j}||_{p}}=|1+\sum_{j\leq i<n}\tau(f^{i}x)B^{+}(x_{j})|=\frac{\sum_{0\leq i\leq n-1}\tau(f^{i}x_{0})+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{0})}}{\sum_{0\leq i\leq j-1}\tau(f^{i}x_{0})+\frac{1}{B^{+}(x_{0})}}\geq 1

\displaystyle\implies\frac{||dx_{n}||}{||dx_{j}||}\succsim\frac{\cos\phi_{j}}{\cos\phi_{n}}\frac{||dx_{n}||_{p}}{||dx_{j}||_{p}}\succsim\frac{\cos\phi_{j}}{\cos\phi_{n}}\approx\frac{1/n}{1/k^{2}}\succsim\frac{1}{k^{2}}

\displaystyle\implies\diam f^{j}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)\precsim k^{2}\diam f^{n}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)\precsim[\diam f^{n}\big(\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big)]^{1/3},

where the last
``\precsim"
is due to
\diam f^{n}\big[\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\big]=O(1/k^{3})
. We also have that

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{n}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}=|1+\sum_{j<n}\tau(f^{j}x)B^{+}(x_{0})|\approx\frac{n}{\cos\phi_{0}}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{||dx_{n}||}{||dx_{0}||}\approx\frac{\cos\phi_{0}}{\cos\phi_{n}}|1+\sum_{j<n}\tau(f^{j}x)B^{+}(x_{0})|\approx\frac{n}{\cos\phi_{n}}.

Let now
s=\cos\phi_{n}
. Then
|ds|\approx|d\phi_{n}|\approx|dx_{n}|
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})=\int_{f^{n}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})}\frac{s}{n}|dx_{n}|\approx\int_{f^{n}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})}\frac{s}{n}|ds|\succsim\frac{1}{n}(\int_{f^{n}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})}|ds|)^{2}\approx\frac{[\diam f^{n}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{2}}{n}.

Since
\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})=O(1/n^{2})
, then

\displaystyle\diam f^{n}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\precsim[n\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{1/2}\precsim[\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{1/4}.

By combining now all the arguments above we obtain

\displaystyle\diam f^{j}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\precsim[\diam f^{n}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{1/3}\precsim[\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{1/12}.

If
i\in[0,n)\setminus\{j\}
, then, by making use of the relation
\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})=O(1/n^{2})
, we get

\displaystyle\frac{||dx_{i}||_{p}}{||dx_{0}||_{p}}=|1+\sum_{j<i}\tau(f^{j}x)B^{+}(x_{0})|\precsim\frac{n}{\cos\phi_{0}}

\displaystyle\implies\frac{||dx_{i}||}{||dx_{0}||}\approx\frac{\cos\phi_{0}}{\cos\phi_{i}}|1+\sum_{j<n}\tau(f^{j}x)B^{+}(x_{0})|\precsim\frac{n}{\cos\phi_{i}}\precsim n

\displaystyle\implies\diam f^{i}\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\precsim n\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})\precsim[\diam\gamma^{u}(x_{0})]^{1/2}.

So far we proved Hölder continuity for the case when
\phi_{1}<\phi_{Q}
. Actually this argument is analogous to the one for a series of reflections off the flat sides in stadium billiards. If
\phi_{1}>\phi_{Q}
, then all
\phi_{i}

i\in[0,R(x_{0}))
are uniformly bounded away from
0
and
\pi/2
. Then the argument is the same as for bouncing on the flat sides in stadium billiards. Actually, this case is much easier, and we skip a proof. Therefore we obtain Hölder continuity of unstable manifolds. For stable manifolds the argument is similar and, basically, the same as the one for stadium billiards. Thus we do not repeat it here.

Therefore, we obtain the following result.

Corollary 5

Theorem
2.1
holds for the class of semi-dispersing billiards considered in this subsection.

Remark 13
.

From the proof of this corollary, it could be seen that the singularities for this class of semi-dispersing billiards have, in a sense, a similar structure as the singularities in the stadium-type billiards. This is a reason why these two classes of billiards have the same rate of decay of correlations (see
Chernov and Zhang 2008
,
Chernov and Zhang 2005
).
